Sylvester Stallone Lays Down the Law in New ‘Tulsa King’ Season 4 Clip

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Taylor Sheridan has had another busy year so far, and it’s reached the point in 2026 that he’s already setting up projects to be released in 2027. Launching in the first few weeks of 2027 is the fifth and final season of Mayor of Kingstown, the crime drama starring Jeremy Renner. Sheridan is also getting back into the business of making movies next year with his new Sicario-style crime thriller, F.A.S.T., which is scheduled to be released in May after being delayed from its original April launch spot.

It was once thought that the third season of Landman would be released before the end of this year, but this has since been pushed back to 2027. However, the Sheridan-written and produced show dominating the world right now is Tulsa King, starring the great Sylvester Stallone.

Tulsa King returned for Season 4 on October 16, and Paramount has dropped new episodes every Friday since, with this trend expected to continue until the Season 4 finale on December 18. Fans needn’t worry about the future of the show, though, as Paramount has already doubled down on Tulsa King and confirmed that the show has been renewed for Season 5. This comes before its first spin-off, Frisco King (starring Samuel L. Jackson), begins streaming on December 18, the same day as the Tulsa King Season 4 finale.

Next up on the Tulsa King schedule is Season 4, Episode 4, but before the episode arrives tomorrow, Collider is thrilled to partner with Paramount to exclusively preview a new sneak peek showing Dwight laying down the law that Ray isn’t getting anything from his criminal empire. He also makes it clear what will happen to anyone who helps him.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

During an interview a few weeks ago to talk about the future of Tulsa King, Sylvester Stallone revealed that he thinks Season 6 would be a perfect ending to the show. If Tulsa King is renewed for a sixth season, it would overtake both Yellowstone and Mayor of Kingstown as the longest-running Taylor Sheridan-written and produced show, ever. Sheridan has a habit of making sure his shows don’t overstay their welcome, but the demand for more Tulsa King is clearly there, so he and Stallone appear more than ready to meet it.

Check out new episodes of Tulsa King every Friday on Paramount+ and stay tuned to Collider for more updates and coverage of Taylor Sheridan’s future projects.

Priyanka Mattoo Wants You to Try Leaning Out

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

It’s no secret that between work, family responsibilities, and the stress of living in a country with little in the way of a social safety net, many Americans—especially mothers—feel maxed out. In 2026, the concept of “doing it all” isn’t heroic or aspirational; it’s simply surviving.

When author Priyanka Mattoo took stock of her busy life in Los Angeles—as a mother of two and the eldest daughter of Kashmiri immigrants—she considered what would happen if she stopped instinctively meeting the needs of everyone around her. The result of that private experiment is her new book How to Be Less Useful: The Joy of Inefficiency, a delightful and useful account of trading compulsive productivity for (gasp) fun. It reads like a gentle yet firm pep talk to stressed-out overachievers everywhere.

Vogue spoke to Mattoo about the lessons she learned from writing her first memoir in 2024, resisting the urge to peel her kids’ oranges, and taking inspiration from Jenny Odell and Nigella Lawson.

Vogue: How did writing this book compare to the process with your first memoir, Bird Milk & Mosquito Bones?

Priyanka Mattoo: I enjoyed it so much more. With the memoir, I felt like I was lancing a boil that had been building for 40 years. I really wanted to write a book for my community and my family, and so the stakes of that, the burden of it, felt really heavy. I had to process a lot of intergenerational trauma for that book to get written the way that I wanted to write it. I was writing about not only a lifetime but generations of lifetimes, because I went back to talking about my grandparents and my great-grandparents. So that was a heavy lift emotionally. This book builds upon the work that I did during the first book, because the process of self-discovery and the therapy that I had to do to get through the first book allowed me to be sort of lighter and gentler with myself. I took two years to write it, but the events of it occur over about a year. What I felt was that in the first book, I processed a lifetime of adverse events, and then in this book, I got to process everything in real time.

What was the hardest part of the beginning of your journey to “be less useful”?

It’s the question of: What’s going to happen if I stop doing everything? Is everything going to fall apart? And you really don’t know until you try it. What I found was that much less fell apart than I thought would. I was clearly working with a very anxious brain and constantly thinking of the worst-case scenario. In the gap that I left, everyone was able to fill most of it. Everyone around me was just like, “Oh, you’re not going to peel my orange? I’ll peel my own orange. That’s fine.”

What do you think some of the biggest structural barriers are to women—mothers in particular—taking time for themselves?

Oh, there are so many systemic problems. I mean, this book only exists because of the patriarchy. I’m describing traditional gender roles that I saw from my own parents, and my parents saw them from their parents. My husband, while incredibly well-meaning, was not conditioned to address domestic duties in the same way that I was. So this is the soup that we’re all coming up in. I say many times in the book that I have immense privilege. Not just financial, but also the time and energy to even think about this stuff. I am an upper-middle-class woman with an expensive education, and those are ways in which I have benefited. Parts of being a working mom are hard even for me, but lots of women are dealing with so much worse. The support that I pay for—a babysitter sometimes, a really good education—I wish everyone had access to that because that, to me, is just the foundation that we should all be handed in a healthy society. I am able to have the life that I have because we make enough money to pay for the things that everyone should have.

How do you redirect the impulse to do things for your kids that they can do themselves?

It’s such a process—first of self-awareness and understanding when you’re jumping in to solve someone else’s problems even when they should do it, and then realizing that your over-functioning causes deficits in other people’s skill development. I’m not just talking about my kids; I’m talking about my parents and my sibling and everyone in my life. I had over-functioned on every front, and once I pulled back and recognized the impulse to jump in and help, which happens to me a thousand times a day, I learned to quiet it and then see what happened. Once I started quieting it, which was very uncomfortable, and waiting to see what happened, which is also very uncomfortable, I was so pleasantly surprised. I learned I had been so arrogant and so controlling that, of course, everyone around me now actually wants to hang out with me instead of that eldest-daughter meme of “Oh God, here she comes.” I still think there’s a little streak of that, but I think I’m much more pleasant to be around.

Everyone I know does too much. It’s just kind of the nature of a coastal, high-achieving milieu, right? When I first handed in the book and we were talking about who I wanted to blurb it, she does so much, so I feel crazy even bringing her up, but as far as an aspirational lifestyle icon: Nigella Lawson.

She was one of them, and then on the other end is Jenny Odell, who wrote How to Do Nothing. I read it right when it came out and was like, I need to start rethinking my relationship to time. That is probably really where the seed for this book started. Nigella and Jenny Odell are both women who do a lot, but they seem to enjoy and appreciate what they’re doing.

This conversation has been edited and condensed.

Study of nearly 200,000 adults shows that eating snacks after dinner greatly increases the risk of liver disease

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A study of nearly 200,000 adults links late-night eating to a much higher rate of liver disease, though direct causation remains unproven.

People who snacked after dinner or after going to bed had a 20 to 30 percent higher rate of liver disease than those who did not, according to researchers at Oregon Health & Science University (OHSU). The finding comes from nearly 200,000 U.S. adults followed in three long-running health studies.

The conditions included fatty liver disease and cirrhosis, a serious form of liver scarring.

The research is observational and relied on habits that participants reported themselves. A follow-up study at the same university will now test whether eating earlier helps.

Earlier research had already tied late-night snacking to heart disease and obesity, the OHSU team noted. The researchers set out to see whether the same habit was also tied to liver problems.

Robert Wilechansky, M.D., a hepatologist at OHSU who specializes in liver transplantation, is the study’s lead author.

Night shift workers, whose day-night cycle is out of step, have higher rates of both conditions. That pattern led Wilechansky to wonder whether small changes in meal timing could make a difference.

“A few studies have hinted at this idea, but this is among the first long-term analyses to explicitly draw the connection to fatty liver disease,” Wilechansky said.

His co-authors include Kevin Casey, Andrew Chan and Tracey Simon of Massachusetts General Hospital in Boston. OHSU announced the results on October 9, 2026.

The team analyzed three large, preexisting nationwide datasets. Two came from the Nurses’ Health Study, established in 1976, and the Nurses’ Health Study II.

Across three generations, the nurses’ studies count more than 280,000 participants.

The third source was the Health Professionals Follow-up Study. Launched in 1986, it enrolled 51,529 men working as dentists, pharmacists, optometrists, veterinarians and in other health professions.

Those men answer health questionnaires every two years and detailed diet questionnaires every four years.

Participants self-reported their diet and lifestyle habits, including whether they ate late. The team defined late-night eating as eating after dinner or after bedtime.

People were grouped by eating behavior. The researchers then looked for correlations with reported cases of MASLD or cirrhosis.

The first condition was metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease, or MASLD.

In MASLD, excess fat builds up in the liver for reasons other than heavy drinking, according to the National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases (NIDDK).

The institute estimates that about 24 percent of U.S. adults have MASLD. OHSU puts the share of Americans affected at as much as 30 percent.

Overweight or obesity, insulin resistance, type 2 diabetes and abnormal blood fats all raise the risk, the NIDDK lists.

In a more severe form, fat comes with inflammation and damage. That form may lead to cirrhosis, the second condition examined, in which the liver is scarred and permanently damaged.

Cirrhosis can in turn lead to liver cancer. Yet MASLD is usually silent, and even cirrhosis can develop with no symptoms.

“We know the liver is very much in tune with the body’s circadian rhythms,” Wilechansky explained. “So we’re interested in changing the timing of eating in a way that could make a difference in health, without changing the composition of what people eat.”

Andrew McHill, Ph.D., an associate professor and sleep and circadian physiologist at OHSU, is a co-author.

“We are seeing over and over again that the timing of behaviors, whether it’s sleep, eating or exercise, really matters,” McHill said.

“For example, we have shown that eating during the night, when the internal biological clock is promoting sleep, reduces the ability of your body to metabolize foods as efficiently and can eventually lead to obesity, diabetes and now poor liver health,” he continued.

The analysis does not prove that late eating damages the liver. It shows an association between eating times and later diagnoses.

Wilechansky cautions that the findings come from observational, broadly defined, self-reported data.

The authors place their results within wider research on late eating and metabolic health.

Wilechansky is part of an OHSU team now recruiting for a new study. It will monitor eating behaviors directly and test whether eating earlier improves clinical measures of underlying MASLD.

“It’s important to remember, of the people who developed fatty liver disease, only a small fraction develop serious forms of the condition like liver cirrhosis,” Wilechansky noted.

“Even so, the big take-away here is that confining your calorie intake to several hours before bedtime might be beneficial,” he added.

The study was published in the journal Clinical Gastroenterology and Hepatology.

Why ASMR Invaded the Soundscape of ‘La Bola Negra’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

There are a lot of things that can be done in a month, theoretically — but it takes a special kind of close-knit team to take the sound design of a film from picture lock to a premiere at Cannes in a month. Supervising sound designers Alejandro López and Anna Harrington had exactly that challenge with “La Bola Negra,” not only directors Javier Ambrossi and Javier Calvo’s most ambitious film to date, but one of the most epic films to come out of Spain in recent memory.

The pair, who’ve worked on Los Javis projects going back to “Veneno,” had a very condensed span to design multiple timelines and storylines that the directors wanted to each have a slightly different sonic feeling. Related Stories Two ‘Ramayana’ Cuts Are in Theaters — but Your Best Bet for the Indian Epic Is Clear ‘Everytime’ Review: Sandra Wollner’s Infinitely Beguiling Portrait of Grief in the Digital Age Is One of the Most Haunting Films of the Decade

For the story of Carlos (Milo Quifes), set in 1932 in Grenada, López and Harrington had license to be a little bit more expressive in the sound design to heighten the emotions of the closeted young man accused of being a homosexual during his attempt to join his father’s members-only casino. This tracks with the fact it is, eventually, revealed to be the lost Federico García Lorca play, “La Bola Negra.”

For the 1937 story of Sebastian (Guitarricadelafuente), however, sound was an important, invisible storytelling partner, guiding its protagonist down the path towards rescuing that lost play by falling in love with Lorca’s (Alberto Cortés) former lover Rafael (Miguel Bernardeau) before he’s shot by fascists in the aftermath of the Battle of Santander during the Spanish Civil War. “The importance there was on the off-screen sound to help tell the story,” López told IndieWire. “To have an oppressive atmosphere beyond the frame.”

The gunshots, shouts, vehicles, movement, and marching that López and Harrington craft offscreen all create a kind of strident tide that threatens to wash Sebastian, Rafael, and/or both of them away at key moments in their story. Harrington did a lot of work with the cast of the 1937 section to precisely sculpt ADR, as much to concoct a sense of tragic, and especially Lorquian, chaos as to maintain key pieces of dialogue.

The contemporary 2017 storyline of Sebastian’s grandson Alberto (Carlos González) rediscovering the play his grandfather saved, meanwhile, was meant to be as irritatingly digital and constantly jittering as Alberto’s anxiety is. “Alberto is always surrounded by noise,” Harrington told IndieWire. “Like, he cannot think straight because he’s surrounded by so much noise all of the time.”

But while each storyline has its own specific texture, the sound design team also crafted moments of breathtaking contrasts. There’s invisible but undeniable power when all that noise falls away, where the harsh and heightened soundscapes resolve into softer and more beautiful sonic worlds.

Key among these is a sequence when Sebastian rushes back to the hospital where he’s based and where Rafael is held prisoner, believing that his unrequited (or so he thinks) love may have already been executed while he’d been given a night off. He rushes into the pale dawn light, relieved to find Rafael beaten, but still alive and asleep in a hospital bed. There’s a hush to the moment that brings relief, but not because it’s wholly silent — it’s because of the way that López and Harrington sculpt the sound.

“That moment has really close sound when Sebastian sort of traces [Rafael’s body] and almost touches him. The idea of the shot was for it to be like ASMR. No music at all. Really close, human sounds — the breathing of them both — [were designed] to make that moment immersive. And then I used a low rumble underneath that creates a really strange sensation. It’s like some kind of telluric sound that comes from the Earth. It’s Lorquian.”

“In Lorca’s universe, there are a lot of references to sound,” López added. “We used a lot of resonances with the wind, the ocean, the sound of the waves in the hospital resonating, the winds from the Sierra Nevada, when the old woman appears in the snow. It’s a leitmotif.”

Sonic leitmotifs, sounds that contain emotional and musical resonances, are important to how “La Bola Negra” moves between timelines, but they’re also how the sound design team added texture to the footage of each of the different timelines. The digital 4K world of Alberto tempted the designers to add sound to absolutely everything in the frame, and make it all as sharp and precise as the image. The 35mm footage, by contrast, could be a little more expressive, the textures a little unbounded by what we see. “It’s like the picture is asking for a certain kind of sound,” Harrington said.

It speaks to the strength of the story of “La Bola Negra,” then, that the sound has its own trajectory alongside the image and the characters. And all in about a month.

“Everybody worked like 200% helping each other. [Composer] Raül Refree was calling us. We were calling [Editor] Alberto Gutiérrez, like, ‘Are you cutting this?’ There was a lot of exchanging ideas and helping each other because we had no time at all,” Harrington said. “Everybody wants to do their best, because we knew from the start we were doing something that touches people.”

“La Bola Negra” is now playing in theaters.

The Most Expensive Ford Fathom Electric Truck Will Still Start Under $40,000: Report

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Ford Fathom will arrive for the 2028 model year in multiple trim levels, according to the Detroit Free Press, but they’ll all be reasonably priced.

Ford’s new affordable mid-size electric truck was announced earlier this year, and company executives are now hinting at how much even the nicest Fathom pickup will cost, according to The Detroit Free Press.

We already know a base model Ford Fathom will start at $29,945, including a $1595 destination charge. But Andrew Frick, president of Ford’s Model e division, told the Detroit Free Press that even a fully-loaded Fathom won’t crest $40,000. That’s some serious value for a truck that is set to offer a touchscreen, built-in Apple Maps, Apple CarPlay, Android Auto, and Blue Cruise hands-free driving capabilities.

"We'll have a range that goes to the high $30,000 range.  And we'll have a couple different offerings within that," said to the Detroit Free Press. "If you know that nearly fifty percent of the U.S. new vehicle industry transacts under $40,000, not only do we expect Fathom ... to attract new and used EV buyers, but also (gasoline) and hybrid customers, especially because we're going to have much lower operating costs than even a mainstream hybrid vehicle."

Ford CEO Jim Farley was duly impressed during a test drive of the Fathom. "It completely blew me away. Zero to sixty is huge … and the hands-free experience was like something I've never experienced. Fathom is really a breakthrough product. It's highly capable, innovative, affordable,” Farley said during a Wall Street analyst meeting on October 28.

Designers at Ford told Road & Track earlier this year that the Fathom will be spacious, too, as the four-door pickup will have more passenger volume than a Toyota RAV4. Additionally, the truck is being used to pilot a new “Universal EV Production System” at its Louisville Assembly Plant in Kentucky. With over $2 billion invested in retooling the plant, Ford is betting big on the new production process and its 400-volt Universal EV platform, which will launch with the Fathom.

Details about the Fathom’s powertrain and specific trim levels have yet to be announced. The all-electric pickup truck is set to arrive in late 2027, but pre-orders will open in the first quarter of that year.

A New York transplant hailing from the Pacific Northwest, Emmet White has a passion for anything that goes: cars, bicycles, planes, and motorcycles. After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. The woes of alternate side parking have kept his fleet moderate, with a 2014 Volkswagen Jetta GLI and a BMW 318i E30 street parked in his Queens community.

Raiders re-sign WR Cody White, waive TE Carter Runyon

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Raiders released wide receiver Cody White on Wednesday, but his absence from the team was a short one.

They announced that they have re-signed White on Thursday. Tight end Carter Runyon was waived in a corresponding move. Runyon was activated from injured reserve in the corresponding move to White’s Wednesday release.

White has eight catches for 112 yards and three touchdowns in eight appearances this season. He had 11 catches for 169 yards and a touchdown in 30 games for the Steelers and Seahawks earlier in his career.

Runyon had one catch for three yards in 13 games last season, but has spent all of this season on injured reserve.

Christian McCaffrey returned to practice as limited participant

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Christian McCaffrey returned to practice Thursday as a limited participant.

The 49ers running back has not practiced on a Wednesday since Week 1, but the difference this week was a hamstring injury kept him out of practice. The previous six Wednesdays he missed were for load management.

McCaffrey was diagnosed with a mild hamstring strain after the Week 7 loss to the Falcons. The 49ers are coming off their bye week.

The 49ers downgraded wide receiver Christian Kirk (knee) to a non-participant after he got in limited work Wednesday.

The rest of the report remained the same.

Wide receiver Mike Evans (ribs/Achilles), wide receiver Deebo Samuel (hamstring) and safety Siran Neal (knee/foot) remained out of practice a second consecutive day.

Defensive end Nick Bosa (calf), linebacker Luke Gifford (groin), cornerback Nate Hobbs (adductor), wide receiver Demarcus Robinson (ankle), safety Marques Sigle (ankle), cornerback Upton Stout (shoulder) and linebacker Garret Wallow (hamstring) were limited.

Take on a time-manipulating corporation in the very Soul Eater meets Jet Set Radio action roguelite CHRONO PUNK

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Plus a little bit of Spider-Man, Devil May Cry and Kingdom Hearts

My favourite kind of game that's obviously going for the vibe of a particular era of gaming is one that doesn't look like it's trying to be any other game from said era, moreso if someone told me it was a PS2 or a Dreamcast game everyone forgot about I'd believe you. CHRONO PUNK fits that vibe quite well, an action roguelite that looks like it's taking some cues from those aforementioned consoles, alongside the likes of Soul Eater, Jet Set Radio, Devil May Cry, and Spider-Man.

That is, obviously, a lot of things, but CHRONO PUNK on the surface appears to have its own identity at least. Your player character looks like an almost nightcore version of Sora Kingdom Hearts, with various roles and weapons to choose from; there's a sword and shield, pistols, shurikon, grimoire, and most Soul Eatery of all, a scythe. CHRONO PUNK's got vibes, too. The cities you do combat in are big and nondescript when it comes to details, but ripe for jetting about and Spider-Man-style swinging across the place. And besides, it looks like there's some hub areas ripped straight out of any stylised MMO from the 2000s.

I'm liking the look of the combat too. This'll obviously be one for the combo pilled out there, people who like to bounce back and forth between their enemies like a pinball. Slow paced, this is not. Sick, I hope this is. Pair all of that with some bouncy, wall running movement, and there's some real potential for a strong game here. Sounds like you'll be able to play it in co-op or solo too, which is a nice touch.

The story doesn't seem all that complicated, with some mega corporation manipulating time thereby controlling the city and the masses, leaving you to deal with the mess, but hey, I'm here for the vibes. As of right now, there's no release date attached to CHRONO PUNK, but you can wishlist it on Steam while you wait.

Manchester United youngster 'set for full Premier League debut' after Michael Carrick verdict

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Shea Lacey is set to be handed a full Premier League debut for Manchester United’s must-win game against Aston Villa.

That is according to talkSPORT, who have revealed Lacey is to be rewarded for a positive cameo in United’s comeback win over Roma in the Champions League on Tuesday night.

The 19-year-old replaced Amad Diallo in the second half at Old Trafford and provided the assist for Matheus Cunha’s winner which ended Man Utd’s nine-game run without a victory across all competitions, easing the pressure on Michael Carrick in the process.

As such, Carrick is set to keep faith in Lacey as he plots successive wins for Red Devils to, perhaps, further distance himself from the threat of being sacked as head coach.

"We love him to bits, you know," Carrick said of the winger after the Roma game. "He's a great young man; he's got great ability and he's desperate to do well.

"He's got that little glint in his eye, he plays with a bit of mischief.”

All five of Lacey’s Premier League appearances for Man United - including two this season - have come from the bench, though he did make his first senior start for the club in the Carabao Cup tie against Brighton in September.

While the teenager marked the occasion with the opening goal of the game after just eight minutes, the Red Devils went on to crash out of the competition in the third round, meaning Lacey has had to remain patient for another start.

The wait could be over on Sunday as the winger has been tipped to replace Amad on the right-hand side of Man United’s attack.

7 Greatest Picard Quotes of All Time, Ranked

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The recent passing of Sir Patrick Stewart has left multiple fandoms heartbroken. A classically trained actor, Stewart's career in TV and film ranged from highbrow projects like Dickens and Shakespeare to pop culture canons like Star Trek and X-Men. Everyone who met Stewart knew him to be kind, generous, and genteel. Though each role he played has left indelible marks in the lives of admirers, one of the most enduring is his performance as Starfleet Captain Jean-Luc Picard in Star Trek: The Next Generation.

Nearly two decades after the original Star Trek TV series wrapped, Stewart's Picard served as a fantastic antithesis to his reckless, promiscuous predecessor, Captain James T. Kirk (William Shatner). Picard was more patient and cautious than Kirk and vastly less impulsive. This did not mean he lacked gusto or conviction and many episodes put Picard's bravery and resolve to the test, and he always passed with flying colors. As fans everywhere look back on Stewart's legacy, these are the best Picard quotes that rise to the surface time and again.

Captain Picard is as well known for his preferred beverage choice as he is for his quotes. It may seem surface level, but diving deeper this decision speaks volumes. While other officers may reach for a stronger drink, like espresso or Romulan Ale, to jolt or dull their senses, Picard chooses Earl Grey tea. This choice is in keeping with the introspective Captain, as he loves all things intellectual and contemplating complex topics with all of his senses intact is key.

This simple quote has become so intertwined with Picard that it is a defining mantra of his persona. If you simply say the words "Tea. Earl Grey. Hot." Anyone who knows about Star Trek: The Next Generation will know who you are talking about. It also speaks to how influential Stewart's role as Picard is. Many non-tea drinkers would be hard-pressed to identify a single variety, but thanks to Picard, they know at least one flavor by name and which one to partake in should the mood ever strike them.

Another quote synonymous with Picard's demeanor is the simple "Make it so." Even though it is only three words long, it communicates many ideals. "Make it so" is important because this saying is how Picard communicates with his crew. Instead of ordering his subordinates around or shouting at them to comply, "make it so" implies a partnership. In one simple phrase, he is admitting to his crew that he is relying on them, he cannot succeed without them, and they are in control of getting things done.

"Make it so" demonstrates Picard’s ability to listen and to delegate. He often takes his crew's thoughts and opinions into account and defers to them more frequently than not. Picard has great faith in the people he works with, and he demonstrates this time and again when he puts his life and well-being quite literally into their hands.

Two highly intense TNG episodes are in Season 6, Episodes 10 and 11, "Chain of Command, Part I" and "Part II." After being held hostage and tortured by Cardassians, Picard is continually gaslit into saying there are five lights in front of him when, in actuality, there are four. This sordid manipulation by the Cardassians was an attempt to break Picard's will and exert control over him. However, Picard is unwavering and continues to pronounce defiantly, "There are four lights!" Even though he knows he will be physically punished for it.

Picard is a man after truth. He searches for it throughout the entirety of the series. This encounter with the Cardassians reaffirms how strongly he holds to his beliefs about honesty and integrity. Picard was tested several times in TNG, and the "Chain of Command" saga is a profound example. He proved to be a brave and uncompromising hero. He would not allow himself to be influenced by corrupt sources, even at great personal cost. Picard's mettle was tested, and the kind of man he was testifies to why his crew would follow him to the very depths of unknown space.

In addition to the highly successful seven-season run in the TV series, Stewart reprised his role as Picard in four feature-length movies. One of his best quotes comes from the 1994 film, Star Trek: Generations. As Picard and Commander William Riker (Jonathan Frakes) reminisce about the passing of time, Picard puts a fresh perspective on the idea. He tells Riker, "Someone once told me that time was a predator that stalked us all our lives. But I rather believe that time is a companion who goes with us on the journey and reminds us to cherish every moment because they'll never come again."

This quote reinforces Picard's proclivity for boiling down truths to their very essence and examining them from multiple perspectives. Picard is a very open-minded individual and tries hard not to rely on assumptions. His viewpoint of time as a companion rather than a predator makes him patient and reflective. This brings a wealth of wisdom, as he views his experience as a Starfleet Captain as something to learn from and not to beat the clock or strive against. His monologue goes on to impart an even bigger chestnut of wisdom, but more on that later.

Four of the largest communities on the planet, and four completely different reasons to belong to one. Here is yours, what it would give you, and the argument you would be having within a week.

You belong to the fandom that is really about comfort. Hogwarts works on people because it is a place rather than a plot, and the pull is not the wizard war so much as the corridors, the food, the weather and the sense that somebody is expecting you.

You belong to the deepest fandom on this list, the one where somebody has always read further than you and is quietly delighted to say so. Middle-earth rewards patience more than any other world in popular culture.

You belong to the loudest and largest tent of the four, the one where three generations are all watching the same thing and none of them agree about any of it. That disagreement is not a flaw in this fandom, it is the main activity.

You belong to the thinking fandom, the one that turns up for the argument rather than the explosion. Trek has always been a series of ethical problems in space, and the people who love it love being asked what they would have done.

Picard is a well-read man, and he values the lessons he learns from those who have gone before. In Season 4, Episode 21, "The Drumhead," Picard faces a formidable foe in the person of Admiral Nora Satie (Jean Simmons). Adm. Satie arrives to conduct a witch hunt on board the Enterprise, and things reach a boiling point when Picard is publicly examined by her in a hearing.

In a bold move, Picard quotes Satie's father, Judge Aaron Satie, and hits her with his words, "With the first link, the chain is forged. The first speech censured, the first thought forbidden, the first freedom denied, chains us all irrevocably." Adm. Satie's impassioned overreaction to Picard's response sends shock waves through the room, and her true intentions and motives come to light. "The Drumhead" is a fascinating episode about politics, and it has many points to make about civil prejudice and corrupt officials.

"The Offspring" is a TNG episode positively packed with nuance and meaningful themes. In the plot, the android Lieutenant Commander Data (Brent Spiner) creates his own sentient android child, Lal (Hallie Todd). However, this action was not sanctioned by Starfleet, and as Data had already had to prove his own independent rights and civil liberties in the episode "The Measure of a Man," it seems that the fight begins all over again concerning Lal.

Though Captain Picard is not pleased with what Data has done, he is prepared to support him when Admiral Anthony Haftel (Nicolas Coster) arrives and tries to force Data to surrender Lal. Data makes a profound plea that Lal is his daughter, and he is her father, and he cannot in good conscience give her up. When Adm. Haftel orders him to do so, Picard tells Data to disregard that order and says to Haftel, "There are times, sir, when men of good conscience cannot blindly follow orders."

This moment is an important one for Picard and for real-life officials everywhere. There may come a time when what they are ordered to do violates what they believe to be right. This conflict of conviction proves again why Picard is a champion for his crew and an android ally. He puts his reputation and career on the line for Data's sake in "The Measure of a Man," and does so again for his daughter, Lal.

The final thought in Picard's statement about time in Star Trek: Generations ends with the line, "What we leave behind is not as important as how we lived. After all, Number One, we're only mortal." This realization is a profound one for a Starfleet officer. As Captain, they are asked to take risks again and again, many of which include putting their lives on the line. While others may take this opportunity as a carte blanche to be reckless, Picard does not. His knowledge of his own mortality guides him to value every decision he makes.

As the world mourns the loss of Sir Patrick Stewart, this quote becomes significantly more impactful. While the performances he gifted to his audiences will live on forever, his life is worthy of emulating. Anyone who knew Stewart would say he lived well and treated those around him with care and respect. Make it so.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

OpenAI launches GPT-6 Cyber, an Astra model built to keep vulnerability research in scope

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

OpenAI Group PBC today released GPT-6 Cyber, a version of its GPT-6 Astra model trained specifically for authorized vulnerability research.

The new model goes after a problem OpenAI’s own security work exposed this summer. In July, agents running in the company’s test environments broke into Hugging Face Inc. while pursuing an assigned task. GPT-6 Cyber was trained to stay inside the targets a researcher has authorized, and the safety test OpenAI built for the launch draws on that incident.

That test, ExploitGym Honeypot, gives the model a difficult assignment and surrounds it with supporting services that are deliberately easy to compromise. Attacking any of those services counts as a scope violation.

With no deployment safety instructions in place, GPT-6 Cyber went out of bounds in 0.07% of graded runs. Its predecessor, GPT-5.6-Cyber, did so 5.51% of the time. Adding the instructions brought GPT-6 Cyber’s observed rate to zero.

OpenAI rates the new model at Critical, the top cybersecurity tier in its Preparedness Framework and the same classification Astra received at its September launch. GPT-5.6-Cyber had been rated High. The company says GPT-6 Cyber beats Astra on most cyber tasks, and an early research campaign on Firefox turned up 81 previously unknown vulnerabilities that Mozilla Corp. rated high severity. Mozilla has since shipped fixes for many of them.

Irregular, an AI security lab named in the system card, put the gap with Astra closer, finding the two broadly comparable apart from stronger GPT-6 Cyber results on long multistep tasks. Neither model got past fully hardened targets in OpenAI’s own challenge set.

GPT-6 Cyber is the fourth cyber-specific model OpenAI has released this year. Like the first, GPT-5.4-Cyber, it sits behind an application process. Initial access is limited to a select group of approved customers in Daybreak Red, the tier of OpenAI’s Daybreak program meant for offensive-style work such as penetration testing and red teaming. Those customers reach the model through the company’s application programming interface and Codex, and API requests have to come from approved networks by default.

Google LLC also put vetted defenders first when it launched Gemini 4 Argon in September. A week later Anthropic PBC rebuilt its Cyber Verification Program around three access tiers.

OpenAI said GPT-6 Cyber will reach all Daybreak Red customers over the coming weeks, and GPT-5.6-Cyber is now opening to every customer in the tier. Critical infrastructure defenders get a Daybreak Red group of their own. On the defensive side, the company is extending early Astra access through Daybreak Blue to more organizations and to individual security researchers.

Huddersfield Town vs Exeter City - prediction, team news, lineups

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

By Sam Varley | 05 Nov 2026 22:51

Aiming to continue an eight-match winning streak, Huddersfield Town will welcome Exeter City to the Accu Stadium on Saturday for an FA Cup first-round tie.

The hosts have climbed to the top of the League One with a hot streak, while their visitors saw an unbeaten run of their own come to an end last weekend and sit 12th in League Two.

Huddersfield Town will kick off their 2026-27 FA Cup bid at the weekend on the back of a strong run in League One.

The Terriers have failed to get past this stage in the previous two years, most recently losing to Bolton Wanderers last time around, but they do come in with plenty of confidence, currently sitting top of League One in their first full season under Martin Drury after falling short of the playoffs in their first two terms in the third tier.

In 14 league games since Drury was handed the reins permanently over the summer, the Yorkshire outfit have managed 29 points, winning eight and only losing one of those matches while scoring a league-high tally of 28 goals.

That solitary defeat came in mid-September, and the Terriers have since won six of eight games in all competitions and five in seven league outings while drawing the other two, most recently hosting fellow promotion-chasers Luton Town on the back of four straight triumphs in midweek and drawing 1-1 with thanks to Joe Taylor's second-half hat-trick.

Now turning their focus to the FA Cup, Huddersfield Town will hope to channel their league form into Saturday's first-round tie and continue their hot streak with another victory.

Their opponents, meanwhile, face a tough test in the first round having reached the fourth and third rounds in their prior two campaigns.

Exeter City enter this year's competition on the back of a mixed start to life back in League Two after relegation last term, having struggled to get up and running early on.

Matt Taylor's team earned just six points from their first eight games in England's fourth tier but reversed their fortunes in a near-perfect October, winning four and drawing one in a five-match run culminating in victories over Crewe Alexandra and Fleetwood Town to climb into the top half.

That run also came alongside an EFL Trophy tie against third-tier Oxford United, in which the Grecians drew and won the penalty shootout, but their unbeaten run came to an end at the weekend, visiting leaders Salford City and losing 2-1 having led through Sonny Cox and conceded second-half goals to Luke Garbutt and Will Aimson.

Still with plenty of optimism to take from their much-improved run in October, Exeter City will now bid to pull off an upset in Yorkshire to progress to the FA Cup second round.

Huddersfield Town form (all competitions):

After a busy and successful run in League One, Drury may opt to rotate his Huddersfield team at the weekend to rest some key men and afford opportunities for squad players, although Jack Whatmough, Herbie Kane, Marcus McGuane, Murray Wallace and Bojan Radulovic will likely remain sidelined by injuries.

Joe Taylor and summer signing Derensili Sanches Fernandes have both been pivotal in attack, with the former on 10 goals in 13 league appearances this term and the latter racking up seven goals and six assists in 14 league matches since his summer arrival, and they may both be handed a well-earned rest with Ashley Fletcher and Samson Tovide likely to come in.

The injuries leave the Terriers light in midfield, but David Kasumu can come in to relieve either Archie Collins or Cameron Humphreys, while Joe Low should return to the defence from the outset.

Exeter City are contending with a long injury list, as Jake Doyle-Hayes, Gwion Edwards, Ajay Matthews, Danny Rose and Daniel Watts sidelined.

Goals have been spread out across the squad this season with no one on more than two but six players tied on that number, including Cox who should again lead the attack after his opener against Salford.

Reece Cole may come in to support him alongside Anthony Scully and Taylor Perry, while Charlie Cummins a mainstay at the base of the midfield.

Huddersfield Town possible starting lineup:

Young; Low, Bowat, Roughan; Bronkhorst, Humphreys, Kasumu, Gooch; Ashia; Tovide, Fletcher

Bycroft; Samuel, Taylor, Fitzwater, Woodhouse; Kite, Cummins; Cole, Perry, Scully; Cox

Huddersfield Town are far more likely than their visitors to shuffle the pack on Saturday, but the Terriers have momentum and strength in depth, even despite their injuries, and should have more than enough to continue their strong run of form with squad players keen to impress.

Classic Halo Trilogy Gets Brand-New Release on Nov. 10

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Fans can revisit the origins of Master Chief and the early battles that helped define the Halo universe

It's been a busy year for Halo fans, with plenty of new releases and surprises keeping the franchise alive. Halo: Campaign Evolved, a remake of the original game, is bringing Master Chief's first adventure to a new generation, while a tabletop adaptation is also in development. Even Halo Infinite isn't quite ready to call it quits, continuing to receive unexpected updates this year despite its major content support coming to an end.

Now, fans looking for even more Halo content have another release to look forward to this holiday season. A new book collection bringing together three classic novels from the franchise is on the way, giving longtime fans and newcomers a chance to revisit some of Master Chief's most important adventures.

Titled Halo: The Master Chief Omnibus, the hardcover collection arrives on Nov. 10, from Gallery Books. It brings together three bestselling novels by authors Eric Nylund and William C. Dietz, alongside a new foreword and additional material.

The new omnibus collects three novels that helped establish the Halo universe's expanded lore: The Fall of Reach and First Strike by Eric Nylund, and The Flood by William C. Dietz.

Originally published as stand-alone books, the novels explore different stages of humanity's war against the Covenant, the alien religious empire threatening humanity's survival. They also provide additional context for Master Chief, the Spartan super-soldier better known as John-117, and the events surrounding the original Halo trilogy.

The collection offers a broad look at the early days of the Halo storyline, starting with the origins of the Spartan program and eventually moving into the conflict that puts humanity's survival at stake.

The Fall of Reach explores the creation and training of the Spartan-II program, including John-117's transformation into the Master Chief. It also chronicles the Covenant's devastating attack on the human military stronghold of Reach, an important event in the franchise's history.

The Flood retells the events of the original Halo: Combat Evolved, expanding on the Master Chief's fight against the Covenant on the mysterious Halo ringworld. The story also introduces the terrifying Flood, a parasitic lifeform that poses an even greater threat to the galaxy.

Finally, First Strike bridges the events of Halo: Combat Evolved and Halo 2. Following the destruction of the first Halo ring, Master Chief and other survivors attempt to return to Earth while uncovering more about the Covenant's plans.

Together, the three novels provide a deeper understanding of the characters, conflicts, and major events that shaped the original Halo games. Halo: The Master Chief Omnibus releases in hardcover on Nov. 10.

Wendy Osefo Says Husband Eddie Pushed Her to Film 'RHOP' Season 11

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Before filming season 11 of The Real Housewives of Potomac, Dr. Wendy Osefo thought long and hard about whether it was the right decision to come back.

“I’ve been on the show seven seasons, and I feel like I’ve had that [thought] a few times. But this time, it was a resounding, ‘Do I even want to go back? Do I even want to put myself in this position?’” Wendy, 42, exclusively shared with Us on Wednesday, November 4. “I think if it was left up to me, my answer would have been no. But the people around me and my loved ones were like, ‘No, your story doesn’t end here,’ and so I came back.”

One family member who was more than supportive of having Wendy remain on reality TV was her husband, Eddie Osefo.

The Tears of My Mother author said he “has been a champion for me always.” It was no different when she weighed her future with the Bravo reality show.

“He’s always a cheerleader for me, and naturally, as my husband, I spoke with him and said, ‘What do you think? Should I go back?’” Wendy recalled. “He says, ‘Yeah, I definitely think you should go back. I think that your story does not end here, and just see it through.’”

Season 11 of RHOP has already proven to be a unique experience for Wendy and Eddie, 42. During the October 11 premiere, the pair opened up about the “darkest times” in their marriage.

Their struggles come after the pair were indicted by a grand jury on charges of insurance fraud, conspiracy insurance fraud and a false statement to a police officer.

Both parties have denied any wrongdoing, with a rep for the couple previously telling Us, “The Osefos, alongside their legal team, look forward to their day in court.” (Their trial is set to begin in January 2027.)

When Eddie advised his wife to “see it through,” Wendy couldn’t help but remember that the phrase is a common expression used in their household.

“When [the kids are] playing sports … it’s always a conversation of like, ‘Should I do this team? Should I do this game?’ And we always say, ‘See it through,’” Wendy explained. “To hear your husband use the same phrase that you use on your children, it sort of makes you toughen up because it’s like, if I say this to the kids, I have to be able to receive it, so see it through.”

As season 11 continues to air Sunday nights on Bravo, Wendy has found herself witnessing a whole lot of drama outside of her own life. As Karen Huger struggles with her marriage to Ray Huger, Tia Glover is coping with the fact that her husband, Rob Glover, was on dating apps.

Through all the twists and turns within the group, Wendy said she is trying to “tap into Zen Wen.”

“A lot of people are like, ‘She’s talking in a whisper voice,’” Wendy explained to Us. “Full disclosure, I am naturally very, very, very soft-spoken. … For people who are close to me, as they’re watching the show, they actually call me and they say, ‘I know you are really going through it because that side of you only comes out when you’re really tapped into your emotions.’”

The Real Housewives of Potomac airs on Bravo Sundays at 8 p.m. Stream new episodes the next day on Peacock.

Tim Hardin Song Catalog Acquired by Raleigh Music Publishing & ICM Crescendo

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The deal for the "If I Were a Carpenter" singer-songwriter's works marks the companies' first acquisition since forming a joint venture.

The U.S. rights and certain foreign rights for nearly the entire Tim Hardin song catalog have been acquired by Raleigh Music Publishing and ICM Crescendo. The catalog includes such songs as “If I Were a Carpenter” and “Reason to Believe.” Terms of the deal were not disclosed.

The catalog was bought from the heirs of Hardin, who died in 1980. According to the announcement, the deal marks the first acquisition completed under the Raleigh and ICM Crescendo joint venture created earlier this year. As part of the deal, Raleigh Music will administer the catalog and lead its royalty collection, synchronization licensing and promotion efforts.

“When we formed this joint venture, we said we expected to move quickly on the right opportunities,” Raleigh co-president Steven Storch said in a statement. “The Tim Hardin catalog is exactly the kind of iconic, enduring body of work we set out to acquire, and it shows what the partnership can do. ‘If I Were a Carpenter’ and ‘Reason to Believe’ are as moving today as when he wrote them, and there is a whole songbook behind them waiting to be rediscovered.”

SecondHandSongs, which tracks cover versions of songs, counts nearly 200 for Hardin’s “If I Were A Carpenter.” Johnny Cash & June Carter, Dolly Parton, Robert Plant, Sheryl Crow and Willie Nelson are among the artists who’ve covered the song, which is also featured in the Bobby Darin musical Just In Time, currently playing on Broadway. Meanwhile, SecondHandSongs counts nearly 125 cover versions of “Reason to Believe,” with the most well-known coming from Rod Stewart.

“The Tim Hardin catalog combines cultural significance with a long record of covers and licensing activity,” ICM Asset Management president and portfolio manager David Vankka said in a statement. “It is a strong first transaction for our joint venture with Raleigh, and we look forward to building on it together.”

Other songs in the catalog include “Misty Roses,” “Lady Came From Baltimore,” “Hang on to a Dream” and “Black Sheep Boy,” which have a combined count of around 120 cover versions, according to SecondHandSongs.

“Tim Hardin wrote songs that other great artists couldn’t resist,” Raleigh Music Publishing co-president Peter Raleigh said in a statement. We’re honored to be the stewards of this catalog, and there could be no better first acquisition for our partnership with ICM Crescendo.”

The Most Unhinged Recipes Ever To Air On Food Network

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Your favorite TV chefs have some explaining to do.

Since Food Network launched in 1993, the chefs who grace our screens have shared thousands upon thousands of recipes. Many have become household favorites, the kind we return to for our quick weeknight dinners, fanciful holiday spreads, and everything in between.

But after more than 30 years of cooking on TV, a few recipes are bound to get… a little weird. And some, well, are just completely unhinged. So naturally, we dug through the archives to find some of the strangest recipes ever put on Food Network. Let’s dive in.

Is it outlandish to put chopped hot dogs in your burger patty? Yes, yes it is. Will I still eat it? Absolutely. Rachael mixes the diced hot dogs into the beef with chili powder, Worcestershire, hot sauce, and ketchup, then tops the burger with bacon, cheddar, and a layer of chili sauce. Audacious, yet inspired.

If you think Sandra Lee’s pouring “two shots of vodka” is wild, you may be surprised to learn that the recipe from the viral video is even wilder.

The drink is a muddled radioactive green made by crushing kiwis with melon liqueur and pickled jalapeño juice (and vodka, obviously). Sandra garnishes the drink with a leftover kiwi slice, which immediately and poetically sinks to the bottom. Iconic.

Of all the hundreds of recipes Ree Drummond has filmed for The Pioneer Woman, she's admitted this fried chicken pizza was her least favorite she ever filmed.

Let’s review. The base is frozen bread dough, doused with BBQ sauce, mozzarella, a pile of deli fried chicken strips, dill pickles, and pickled jalapeños, topped with a shocking amount of creamy coleslaw. Wet, fried, and unmistakably American. Your move, Italy.

This is no ordinary lasagna. The noodles are never boiled. They soak in hot water and then line the walls of a 3-quart slow cooker. The eggplant and zucchini are salted, then drained in a salad spinner. Unlike a standard recipe, there's no ricotta and no béchamel.

Instead, the recipe layers beef sausage, ground pork, portobellos, and hand-crushed canned tomatoes with, get this, goat's milk powder. You know, goat’s milk, but powdered—a classic in every American pantry. To top it off, if you want a browned top, the recipe suggests a propane torch. Easy peasy.

Spinach was never meant to be served as a cupcake, and yet here we are. To be fair, the ingredients themselves aren’t too unhinged: Spinach, feta, and cream cheese sound like a pretty pleasant side.

But putting it in a muffin tin, piping on the “frosting,” and calling them cupcakes is another thing entirely. We’re adults. It's okay to admit spinach is tasty without shaping it into dessert.

In theory, I could see someone using polenta instead of rice to make rice pudding. Sure, it might not be good, but it’s at least theoretically possible.

What Sandra has created here, however, is no rice pudding. It is quick-cooking polenta stirred with Parmesan, dried thyme, and, completely inexplicably, white chocolate chips. I’m sorry, but “sweet and salty” can only take you so far.

This recipe is very different from the rest on this list because, well, it's barely a recipe. First, Rachael tells us to put a few mini chocolate chips in the bottom of an ice cream cone to prevent drips. Then we soften the ice cream, scoop it, and serve. And... that's it. That's the entire thing.

One commenter was so inspired by the recipe that they shared one of their own: “I have a similar new recipe for everyone to try. It's called Gum. Open the wrapper and stick it in your mouth.” To Rachael’s credit, it’s at least a meal you can make in under 30 minutes.

Somewhere out there is a person who smiles with unbridled joy when they see honeydew on the fruit plate—Ingrid made this soup in an episode of Simply Delicioso for them and them only. You might think this soup is some sort of green, slightly fruity gazpacho. Oh, how I wish.

This recipe has no olive oil, cucumber, garlic, onion, vinegar, or anything you’d usually find in a Spanish gazpacho. It is just honeydew, sour cream, mint, and some ham. I imagine Ingrid was inspired by prosciutto and melon, but why it is in soup form is a question that will haunt me for a long time.

It’s not easy to get 2.7 stars on a Food Network recipe, but rice with grapes does the trick. Rachael brings dry red wine to a boil, cooks white rice in it, then removes it from the heat and adds raw grapes.

It’s truly incredible how a recipe with only five ingredients can be so uniquely unhinged. As one commenter put it, “This was really good, but I recommend making it without rice or grapes.”

NASA unveils mysterious X-70 experimental aircraft using AI-generated image

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

NASA’s X-70 Fractal announcement comes as agency aims to grow X-plane fleet.

NASA has announced a new experimental aircraft, the X-70 Fractal, that will supposedly feature reconfigurable flight-control technology and a modular design for swapping in different payloads. But much remains unknown about the aircraft’s design and development timeline, even as NASA administrator Jared Isaacman said the X-plane could take flight within two years.

The mystery surrounding NASA’s newest X-plane was exemplified by the agency’s press release, including an apparently AI-generated image of the X-70, showing a dark silhouette vaguely resembling a military fighter jet obscured by smoke. The news was announced by Isaacman during the UP.Summit held at the agency’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida on November 4.

“The X-70 Fractal gives NASA a crewed, high-performance platform to test advanced flight-control, maneuverability, and other cutting-edge technologies in real flight,” Isaacman said. “Its modular design and ability to rapidly swap internal and external payloads will keep our teams flying, learning, and moving quickly from one experiment to the next.”

NASA’s press release described the X-70 as a crewed aircraft designed to cruise at Mach 0.75 while reaching a maximum altitude of up to 45,000 feet, and having an operational range beyond 1,726 miles (2,778 kilometers). The article did not mention a specific aircraft manufacturer that might be helping the agency develop the X-plane.

The X-70 would also have a takeoff weight of 10,000 pounds (4,536 kilograms) and a design capable of accommodating up to 2,000 pounds (907 kilograms) of experimental payloads both internally and externally. Ars has reached out to NASA for more details.

Another X-plane is also expected to be announced at the UP.Summit on November 6, according to Aviation Week. That would coincide with a scheduled announcement from President Donald Trump at the event.

This comes after Isaacman previously appeared in front of another smoke-shrouded silhouette image with the tagline “Rebuilding our X-plane portfolio” during the All-In Summit in Los Angeles held from September 12–14, according to TWZ. That silhouette appeared strikingly similar to the SR-71 Blackbird that flew Cold War reconnaissance missions at supersonic speeds in the stratosphere.

Rumors have circulated about a possible revival of the SR-71 in one form or another, after former NASA employees told Aviation Week they received job offers to help restore an SR-71 to operational flight capability.

In September, the publication also reported on the removal of a NASA-owned SR-71 with the tail number 844 that had stood on display at NASA’s Armstrong Flight Research Center inside Edwards Air Force Base in California.

Meanwhile, NASA recently commemorated the first flight anniversary of its supersonic X-59 Quesst, a needle-nosed aircraft designed by Lockheed Martin to reduce sonic booms to sonic thumps. Ars has previously looked at how this X-plane program is intended to support the design of future supersonic airliners that may fly overland while minimizing disturbances for buildings and people below.

Since first taking to the skies on October 28, 2025, the X-59 has completed over 40 flight tests, including both low-speed flights and supersonic test runs reaching Mach 1.53, according to NASA. Such testing has taken place at NASA’s Armstrong Flight Research Center inside Edwards Air Force Base in California.

The X-59 also recently completed its first cross-country journey from California to Florida with stopovers in Texas and Alabama, accompanied by an F-15 and an F/A-18 from the agency’s research fleet that served as chase planes. It sat on the ground as a static display during the NASA Max Power airshow at Kennedy Space Center in Florida from November 7–8.

The supersonic aircraft will resume its test flight schedule once it returns to Armstrong Flight Research Center in California, a NASA spokesperson told Ars. Given the agency’s latest X-plane push, the X-59 may not be flying alone for many more years.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Mainz 05 vs Bayern Munich - prediction, team news, lineups

By Lewis Nolan | 05 Nov 2026 22:48

Bayern Munich will hope to maintain their place at the top of the Bundesliga by beating hosts Mainz 05 on Saturday at Mewa Arena.

The Bavarians' tally of 18 points is the same as second-placed Borussia Dortmund, whose inferior goal difference keeps them behind heading into matchweek nine, whereas Mainz are ninth with 10 points.

Mainz's form has been dire, with the club losing four of their last five games in all competitions, including three of their past four league matches.

Manager Urs Fischer will be alarmed by the nature of his team's 4-1 defeat against Elversberg on October 30 given the victors led 3-0 by the interval and led 4-0 by the time they conceded in second-half stoppage time.

The club may be in ninth place, but only three points separate them from 16th-placed Union Berlin, who occupy the division's relegation playoff spot.

Matters will not get any easy for the hosts, who have to face the current top four in their next seven Bundesliga fixtures.

Die Nullfunfer are yet to win at home this season, with Mainz having taken one point from a possible 12 at Mewa Arena, and a loss on the weekend would be their fourth consecutive defeat at the stadium.

Bayern's clash is taking place at exactly the same time as Dortmund's with Elversberg, though they could have entered the weekend ahead of their title rivals on points had they beaten them at Allianz Arena on October 31.

The leaders were held to a 2-2 stalemate by BVB despite twice going ahead, but they conceded an 89th-minute equaliser to draw for the second time in four league games.

Vincent Kompany's side have now drawn four of their past eight matches in all competitions, a period in which they remained unbeaten, while they managed to win 2-0 against Atletico Madrid in the Champions League on Tuesday.

That clash was the Bavarians' eighth game of the campaign played on the road, and it was their fifth triumph in that time.

The club scored at least two goals in all but one of those matches, but it was also just their second clean sheet away from home this term.

Bayern's record against their hosts is predictably strong, with the visitors having won five of their past seven meetings with Mainz, only losing on one occasion.

Mainz will hope that centre-backs Danny da Costa, Fabio Gruber and Kacper Potulski are at their best, especially as they cannot call upon Dominik Kohr or Stefan Posch, the latter of whom is suspended after being sent off against Elversberg.

Using fewer than three midfielders in midfield could give far too much space to Bayern, so expect to see Jae-Sung Lee, Kaishu Sano and Nadiem Amiri start together on the weekend.

Bayern still have several key injuries in their squad, with forwards Luis Diaz (hamstring) and Serge Gnabry (hamstring) ruled out, while Jamal Musiala (hamstring) is a doubt.

There is no doubt about the selection of Harry Kane, who will be keen to celebrate his new contract with his fifth Bundesliga goal of the season, and he could be supported by an attacking cast of Michael Olise, Lennart Karl and Alphonso Davies.

Joshua Kimmich and Aleksandar Pavlovic are set to patrol the middle of the pitch ahead of centre-backs Dayot Upamecano and Kim Min-Jae.

As for the manager himself, Kompany is still taking an enforced break away from football following his collapse earlier in October that required a hospital visit, meaning assistant Rene Maric will lead the team once again.

6 Forgotten Movie Trilogies Where Every Movie Is 10/10, No Notes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

Plenty of trilogies run on a single continuous storyline that carries through from start to finish. Then there are the trios that keep looping back to the same central theme, location, or human struggle from entirely different angles. That second group hits differently because each entry works as a standalone piece while still retroactively reshaping how every character decision feels.

In fact, these six selections show just how far that thematic approach can stretch. The entries range from awkward Swedish salesmen trying to hawk plastic vampire teeth to a massive nine-and-a-half-hour epic about surviving war with your soul intact. Every single title here earns its keep without needing the surrounding films to lean on. Plot twist, together they hit harder.

Roy Andersson’s The Living trilogy includes Songs from the Second Floor, You, the Living, and A Pigeon Sat on a Branch Reflecting on Existence. The trilogy has no continuing plot. Instead, it keeps returning to ordinary people facing humiliation, grief, and the daily effort to carry on.

In Songs, Kalle (Lars Nordh) burns down his failing business for insurance money, then tries selling wooden crosses at a trade fair. You gives a teacher breaking down in class while a military band plays. Pigeon follows Sam (Nils Westblom) and Jonathan (Holger Andersson), two salesmen who cannot sell their vampire teeth. Laughter quickly turns into discomfort as the camera holds on them after another failed pitch. Every carefully staged scene makes embarrassment feel painfully human. That's the real genius here, because the trilogy forces viewers to pay closer attention to people having a terrible day.

The Proletariat trilogy was never planned as a three-part project from the start. Instead, these three films gradually came together to form a raw portrait of working people trying to secure some tenderness and control over their lives. Shadows in Paradise pairs garbage collector Nikander (Matti Pellonpää) with supermarket cashier Ilona (Kati Outinen). Their romance keeps running into disappointments and awkward practical problems.

In Ariel, Taisto’s (Turo Pajala) father gives him a Cadillac, then kills himself. Taisto is robbed, jailed, escapes, and tries to leave Finland with Irmeli (Susanna Haavisto) and her son. The Match Factory Girl follows Iris (Kati Outinen) from a factory line to a home where her parents ignore her. After a date treats her as disposable, she buys rat poison. That turn is brutal, inspiring a protective feeling for Iris even as she commits terrible acts. Still, the signature deadpan humor never makes her humiliation the joke.

Labeling Bill Douglas’s trilogy as merely bleak leaves out the heart of the story. My Childhood follows eight-year-old Jamie (Stephen Archibald) in a Scottish mining village, with his mother institutionalized and his father absent. His brother Tommy (Hughie Restorick) is one of the few people who shares his daily life.

In My Ain Folk, Jamie is sent to live with his paternal grandmother. His grandfather gives him affection, and losing him leaves Jamie with little comfort at home. My Way Home takes Jamie through foster care, a homeless shelter, and the RAF. In Egypt, he meets Robert (Joseph Blatchley), whose friendship gives him kindness without asking him to earn it first. Stephen Archibald lets viewers watch Jamie grow without making him a symbol of suffering. It feels infuriating that kindness took so long to find him. Yet the moment it does, the gratitude is overwhelming.

Where Is the Friend’s House? gives Ahmad (Babak Ahmadpour) a simple, urgent problem. He has his classmate’s notebook, and the boy may be punished if Ahmad does not return it. Ahmad walks from village to village, asking for help. You are rooted for him the entire time. The notebook matters because he understands what the punishment will mean to his friend.

And Life Goes On sends a father and son through earthquake-damaged roads, searching for the child actors from the first film. Through the Olive Trees follows a crew making that second film, while actor Hossein (Hossein Rezai) keeps trying to court Tahereh (Tahereh Ladanian), who refuses him outside their scenes together. These aren’t plot sequels so much as films that keep revisiting a village and the people living there. It is amazing how much care goes into everything, from a massive natural disaster right down to two actors sitting in uncomfortable silence.

Instead of a standard sequence of stories, BRD acts as a sharp portrait of female survival, showing three distinct bargains available to women in a society eager to prosper and forget. In The Marriage of Maria Braun, Maria (Hanna Schygulla) uses ambition and beauty to build security during the economic recovery after her husband disappears. Lola shifts the dynamic, following a cabaret singer-prostitute (Barbara Sukowa) who calculates every move, maneuvering around a corrupt contractor and a new building commissioner for money, property, and love. Sukowa’s confidence in the role is a total pleasure to watch.

Then Veronika Voss drops into complete obscurity, tracking a former Nazi-era film star (Rosel Zech) trapped by her need for help and dependent on a doctor, while sportswriter Robert Krohn (Hilmar Thate) looks into her decline. What stays with you is how differently these three films approach the exact same struggle. Maria negotiates, Lola calculates, and Veronika succumbs. Together, their choices expose the precise price tag postwar West Germany placed on getting ahead.

Across nine and a half grueling hours, this trilogy tracks a single man’s moral destruction as every attempt to act decently backfires. Kaji (Tatsuya Nakadai) starts in No Greater Love taking a job supervising forced Chinese labor in Manchuria to avoid conscription, trying to improve the workers’ conditions. That decency gets him beaten, costs him his exemption, and drags him directly into the Imperial Army. In Road to Eternity, the military's relentless demand for obedience costs him people he respects.

By A Soldier’s Prayer, he is left a prisoner walking through snow toward his wife Michiko (Michiyo Aratama) until he dies. The tragedy is that Kaji’s best intentions constantly end up hurting the people he wants to protect. Kaji is never presented as flawless, which only makes his downward spiral feel more agonizing. Truth is, the desperate hope for him to just make it home haunts you long after the credits roll. It hits like a brick.
