According to a WIRED analysis, by Wednesday morning, candidates backed by Musk's America PAC had lost 20 races and won only 1. There are still several that haven't been called yet, but to put another way with where we're at right now with the information we have, of every traceable dollar that Musk put into a race that has now been decided, more than 99 cents went to a race that he lost. Just unbelievable figures there.

Vittoria Elliott: I mean, I think it's pretty incredible that two years ago, America PAC was this new force in Republican politics. Everyone was starting to play to the sort of America PAC donation situation, and it pulled in not just money from Musk, it pulled in money from big VCs like Shaun Maguire. And in 2024, as we reported, their ground game wasn't exactly super strong, but it was just the willingness to pump money in. Similar to how VCs make things work, you pump money in until your team wins. And just to watch that strategy really crash and burn, but I think we saw some early indications that, particularly in the months post DOGE, that Elon himself became a really toxic thing for Republicans to start attaching themselves to.

Vittoria Elliott: And so then it became this sort of monkey's paw situation where it's like you can take the America PAC money and then you get tarred with the Musk brand. And those two things, maybe at best they cancel each other out, but it seems like actually the Musk brand is more toxic than he and his candidates could buy their way out of.

Leah Feiger: I 100 percent agree with you. Hugo, you were very involved in the WIRED tracking project where we built a live tracker of Musk's political spending. Can you take us through a couple of the candidates that he put all of this dough behind and how they ended up doing?

Hugo Lowell: Yeah, look, in total, Musk's America PAC spent about close to $500 million actually, half a billion dollars this midterm cycle, him alone. I mean, if you conceptualize that sort of spending, the way to think about it I think is he effectively ran a shadow Republican national committee. That is the scale of that contribution. And were it not for that money, by the way, some of these losses might've been even greater. Tori talked about the ground game that America PAC financed, that has been a big focus both in '24 and also in this cycle. Obviously his biggest focus was Texas. That is his home base. That was where he spent the most amount of time, the most amount of money and the most amount of resources, and it basically got him nothing. I mean, Ken Paxton lost by such a wide margin that hanging onto James Talarico's coattails was Gina Hinojosa in the governor's race. So I think the way to look at Musk's spending, if you talk to Republican operatives and people in Trump world, is to think about it in terms of what is this money actually buying him? For him, it's a rounding era. Half billion dollars to Elon, it's kind of pocket change, which is kind of incredible to think about, but that is the case. And so what is the net effect he gets from this? And it is probably the fact that he can play kingmaker now running into 2028. And if he decides to back a particular presidential candidate, I'm sorry to say this, we already just finished this election, but '28 is around the corner. If he decides to back a particular candidate, no one else is really going to be able to compete with him on the money front because if he sinks another half billion, if not more, in the next cycle, who else has that kind of money to play with? It's insane.

Leah Feiger: But I think the takeaway, at least in this moment, is that money didn't matter. He couldn't buy these races. People were so mad at Republicans and so mad at Trump's mandate that this didn't actually work out for him. I'm not sure, going into 2028, I would necessarily want Elon Musk behind me.

Hugo Lowell: I don't know that voters necessarily see that though, right? I mean, are voters really tracking which ad was paid for by which super PAC? I understand that the money didn't buy a Texas Senate race. By the way, as it shouldn't, this concept that you can just buy Senate races. And I take your point and I see what you're saying, but it is not clear to me that whether the money would've come from the Senate leadership fund, which is the super PAC aligned with the Senate Republican leadership, or whether it came from America PAC, in some ways doesn't matter. It's like the volume of cash that flowed in, where is that going to be going and what does the future of those kinds of contributions look like? And I do think it kind of flows into '28.

Vittoria Elliott: I would just argue that yeah, maybe if he wrapped the money on many, many layers of PACs that made it hard to trace where it came from, I would agree with you. But anyone who gets money that flows from Elon or even his association with the party allows for someone to run an attack ad that says, "Elon backs this guy. Elon backs this party," they're going to execute on the unpopular will of an unelected billionaire, I think is still going to be toxic even if the money eventually gets routed through the general Republican funding apparatus.

Leah Feiger: I think the though that this is in some ways all a preview for '28 is the right take. Sort of in that vein is where the AI industry showed up in this election cycle as well. Leading the future, which is the pro-AI network backed by Andreessen Horowitz and OpenAI president Greg Brockman, among others. They threw in money for Republican primaries, Democratic ones. They've been sort of all over the place. And to me and to some of the experts people on our team have spoken to, that's really spelled out that they're just kind of slaying the groundwork for '28. They want to see where different, not just races land, but where the population is landing on all of these different issues, which I guess takes us to data centers, a topic that I did not think was going to consume 2026 for me, but here we are. Tori, we have been framing this election quite a lot as the data center referendum. We were not wrong. Across the country, there were 40 ballot measures about AI data center projects. Voters chose to restrict 39 of them, and an NBC exit poll found that almost a quarter of all voters said that data centers were crucial to their vote. Absolutely mind-boggling. Voters hated AI. Voters hated data centers. Talk to us about what you and science senior reporter Molly Taft reported out for WIRED.

Vittoria Elliott: Yeah, so I think a couple of things. First off, I think this sort of ties in many ways to people's resentment of the tech industry and its right word swing more generally, and data centers and AI are sort of the peak embodiment of that. And obviously there's the environmental concerns around data centers, and I think you really saw it as a galvanizing force in rural communities where these data centers are being put, where in communities that really would've more traditionally swung Republican, that it became this really bipartisan uniting effort around the fact that—

Leah Feiger: I can't think of another issue, honestly, that has galvanized.

Vittoria Elliott: I know, something we can all agree on.

Vittoria Elliott: So to go back to South Carolina, for instance, Darline Graham got dubbed Data Center Darline because she was taking money from data center PACs. In Montana with Sam Forstag, who Democratic up and comer, fire jumper, his opponent also tarred and feathered with the data center label in a historically Republican district. So I think we are really seeing this moment where these things are touching down in communities that normally would be Republican, and they're seeing these impacts on their daily lives and having Republican representatives-

Leah Feiger: Or even just the threat of the impact on their daily lives. People were running on, "There could be a data center in your community. Don't worry, I'll stop it from happening." That was Chris Pappas in New Hampshire, for example.

Vittoria Elliott: Totally. And especially in places like Georgia and Texas where a lot of these things are already up and running or are planned, resounding, resounding population telling Republican lawmakers to step off with that. They're not interested. Telling people, "We're going to put this thing in your community. It's going to take up your water. It's going to be loud. It's going to be annoying. It's going to be expensive. It's going to drive up your electricity bills in the middle of an affordability crisis." And then saying to them, "And reward for all of that is it's going to take your job in the middle of a jobs crisis where tons and tons of people are struggling to find work." I just don't think that's a good selling point. And there were individual Republicans who ran saying that they wanted to regulate the AI industry, that they wanted to push back on data centers, but for the Democrats, it really became sort of a whole of party movement around this. And I think that really was rewarded at the ballot box.

Leah Feiger: Yeah. I mean, there were people who brought it into their victory speeches. Jon Ossoff, when he won his Senate race in Georgia, used his victory speech to specifically call out Silicon Valley titans who build bunkers and warned that their AI could cause human extinction. It was pretty incredible to witness. This is a rallying cry, but I wonder if it's going to be still a rallying cry in two years from now. Let's talk about the people who were very, very convinced that no matter what happened this election cycle, it was all going to be fraud. David, you reported out a story on Wednesday about how all of these election deniers who are usually the very, very first to say that something was wrong, that results were inaccurate, there has to be a recount, there has to be investigations, had nothing to say. Election deniers accepted the results. Talk to us about that.

David Gilbert: Yeah. As I was saying earlier, the MAGA crowd, their first reaction was silence and then they started the recriminations. But for election deniers, the silence has continued. There was hundreds of election deniers on the ballots, people who had backed Trump's claims that 2020 was a stolen election. Lots of those races still haven't been called. Some of them have won, but lots of them in their most consequential races have lost. People like Andy Biggs who was running for governor in Arizona or Mike Rogers who was running for Senate seat in Michigan, they both had backed Trump's calls in the wake of 2020 to overturn elections. Jim Marchant was running for Secretary of State in Nevada who was kind of one of the OG election deniers, and he got beaten easily by Cisco Aguilar, who's the incumbent there. But it was just incredible to see when they were making their concession speeches that they didn't mention elections or security or vote rigging or election fraud whatsoever. They didn't mention noncitizens.

Leah Feiger: All of your favorite buzzwords.

David Gilbert: Yeah, they mentioned none of them because in most of the cases, the margins of defeat were so great that it just made no sense. But equally, the groups who are kind of backing this movement, the groups like Cleta Mitchell's Election Integrity Network, True to Vote, people like Patrick Byrne, who's funded much of this, they've just been silent, which makes me wonder, is this kind of the beginning of the end of that movement? Have we seen it better out that they've just realized now that, "No, these elections are secure, it's just people really don't like what Trump is doing and the policies that he has?"

Leah Feiger: Something that you had flagged a number of times in the lead up to the midterms was there were a number of election denier candidates running for attorney general races in states across the country and Secretary of State races. And those two positions don't tend to get a lot of attention, but they're really important because they have to do with how elections are run in those particular states, which would mean that 2028, the next large election coming up, could have been very well impacted had these candidates won their races. So there is this weird stopping point. Does it feel like the end of an era for you, David?

David Gilbert: Well look, I think we probably are talking about it too early because I think in 2024 we thought that maybe it was over because Trump had won so convincingly, so there was nothing for them to complain about and Trump himself didn't bring it up. But we saw that they came back very quickly again, and there was still a lot of money to be made for a lot of these groups because for a lot of people involved in this, it is a grift. They were making money, they were traveling across the country talking to people and getting paid for it. I do think based on the initial response that we've seen that lots of the people who were involved in this are tired. Some of the comments I was reading in some of the Telegram channels that I follow are people are just so tired of this and they seem like they no longer want to take part in it. So it's too early to say that this is the end, but it is possible that a lot of the energy that has been in this movement for six years now will dissipate.

Hugo Lowell: I'm just curious, David, to what extent do you think a lot of this is also follow the leader and the moment Trump came out pretty early on in election night and was like, "I'm going to accept the results." The rest of them thought, "OK, well, if we're not going to have our champion, we're going to stand down too."

David Gilbert: Yeah, I think that's a huge amount to do with it. He hasn't been as active in terms of getting involved in the nitty-gritty of this. He's been making claims that elections are rigged, but I think the movement was kind of self-sustaining, especially in 2025 after Trump had won. So they were able to sustain themselves in that period even when he wasn't promoting them. I do think the fact though that he has so quickly accepted defeats in these midterms and hasn't pushed back, I do think that's going to be a big issue for them and will be difficult for them to kind of get themselves motivated once again.

Leah Feiger: You guys, I want to talk predictions. Midterms, they're over. They're behind us. In January, we're going to have a whole new set of politicians on Capitol Hill, which means it is time to talk about the presidentials. There's a bunch of names that are already floating around, Jon Ossoff, AOC, Gavin Newsom, JD Vance, Marco Rubio. I'm curious, what you think. What are your predictions here? What's going to happen? What should we be looking out for in the next few months?

Vittoria Elliott: I mean, I think one of the big things that we saw a lot of Democrats saying immediately last night was subpoena, subpoena, subpoenas, favorite word. And I think unfortunately that's going to run up against some real friction because as Congress, you can subpoena people, you can try and get them to testify, but ultimately all those federal agencies are under the executive branch. They have political leadership now under the Trump administration that is pretty hostile to Congress. And if you want to enforce that subpoena, you are going to need the DOJ to do that for you. And I can't see AG [Todd] Blanche super excited to assist in that. So I think as much as a lot of these Democrats are coming in saying, "We're ready to do accountability," and I do think they want to, I think the actual mechanisms through which they're going to be able to do that are going to be pretty narrowed, and that's something that they're probably going to have to get ahead of explaining to their constituents right now.

Leah Feiger: Absolutely. Hugo, how about you?

Hugo Lowell: Yeah, just on the presidential question to Democrats, I think it's actually quite a wide open field and you'll have to see how the new dynamics and the new Congress play out. You do have these freshmen Democrats who are going to come in very, very aggressive. And then you also have people like Ossoff who are going to be riding on a wave of a lot of goodwill and a lot of momentum inside the Democratic Party. I think what's more interesting is Republicans because this really screws up a lot of timelines for people, two front-runners that keep getting mentioned, obviously JD Vance as the vice president, and then Rubio to a lesser extent. So on JD, there was a lot of thinking inside Trump world that JD, if it was an OK night, could announce pretty soon after the midterms. He had his donors more or less lined up, but it strikes me that that is now actually going to have to be paused. There is no way a Republican candidate can announce anytime soon with the stench of these midterms coming off on their coattails. It's just not possible. It's just a terrible, terrible political environment for Republicans. And it may only get worse if Trump decides to be an open warfare with Democrats on the Hill, and if he decides to go harder against Iran, that actually conversely might hurt Republicans more. And so I think JD Vance now has a very nuanced and difficult decision as to when he's going to announce, if ever. And as for someone like Marco Rubio, I have always been of the belief, and this is just me talking to people in Rubio's orbit, that he was not particularly enthralled about running this cycle. He saw JD as the heir apparent. There were a lot of movements inside Trump law to make sure that JD got it. And now again, he has a difficult decision. If he decides to run, does he wait for JD to announce and then potentially flame out? Or does he wait and see if he just announce at the same time as JD? Or does he decide actually JD's cooked and now is the time for him to announce, this is the moment for him and he should go right now? I mean, these are all questions that are really unanswerable. And my guess is actually everyone takes a step back, takes stock, see how the next maybe six, 12 months play out before they get going.

Leah Feiger: David, how about you? What do you think?

David Gilbert: Are we definitely ruling out a third term for Donald Trump? Is that off the table? Because Steve Bannon's still talking about it. He's still working on possibilities.

Leah Feiger: I love that this is the area of the internet that you're still steeped in. Obviously the four of us, different areas.

David Gilbert: It's interesting to me because obviously Trump, there's been a split in his base for quite a while now and we're seeing diverge even more after the midterms. But I'd love to know from Hugo actually, how important now, or is it important now, is Trump's endorsement? Because obviously for a long time, Trump was seen as the kingmaker and whoever he endorsed would be the person who would get the nomination. Has that weakened now because of the midterms?

Hugo Lowell: I think for congressional candidates, it's weakened. If you're a senator, if you're a member of Congress who your election coming up in two years time, I think the threat from White House officials, a threat from the president is not going to sway you. And by the way, we are already seeing this start to happen. Seeing people like Lauren Boebert and Thomas Massie be like, "You know what? YOLO, this is going to be my last stint in Congress. I'm going to vote to release the Epstein files." And there was nothing Trump could do sticking Lauren Boebert in a situation room that could change her mind. Asian crypto exchanges batten down the hatches as hacks surge: Asia Express

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Asian crypto exchanges are scrambling to tighten security on fears of a wave of potentially AI-assisted hacks, even as regional governments embrace tokenization and stablecoins.

India’s national cybersecurity agency warned that an AI-assisted threat actor has escalated its attacks on cryptocurrency exchanges and other financial businesses, stealing wallet keys and siphoning funds.

The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) on Wednesday designated the threat actor as “CRAFTY FALCON” and explicitly identified crypto exchanges among affected businesses.

“The threat is active and ongoing. CSIRT-Fin continues to receive reports of successful attacks, including from NBFCs and fintech entities that had already received the earlier restricted advisories and had undertaken remediation,” said CERT-In.

The warning adds to evidence of hackers using AI tools against financial institutions. In October, cybersecurity firm CrowdStrike reported that an unidentified attacker used ARTEX, an AI penetration-testing tool also named in CERT-In’s advisory, alongside Claude Code and other AI models to breach South Korean financial firms and steal data.

South Korea’s Financial Services Commission has urged domestic crypto exchanges to increase the proportion of assets held in cold wallets after the $6.3 billion attack on Binance.

The FSC said it held an emergency meeting with other regulators, cybersecurity agencies and crypto service providers to review security measures.

It reviewed exchanges’ IT access controls, wallet withdrawal approval and signing procedures, and administrator privileges. It also urged providers to increase the proportion of assets held in offline cold wallets. But South Korean law already requires exchanges to hold at least 80% of users’ crypto assets in cold storage, with Upbit keeping 99.17% assets in cold wallets, followed by Bithumb at 94.76% and Coinone at 85%.

The Bank of Korea is surveying 31 financial institutions on how a major exchange shutdown, prolonged crypto downturn or losses at another firm could affect their operations.

One scenario asks how financial firms would be affected if South Korea’s largest crypto exchange by market share temporarily suspended operations following a major hack or similar incident.

Other questions address a steep fall in cryptocurrency prices followed by a sustained trading slump, and the potential fallout if another financial institution suffered heavy losses from a crypto-related incident.

Despite clear evidence of years of North Korea-linked hacking groups stealing billions from crypto exchanges, Pyongyang claims to have had no involvement in the $6.3 billion Binance hack and floated the idea it was actually the Americans.

“The FBI is trying to brand the DPRK as a ‘cyber criminal state’, claiming that it obtained information that a hacking organization ‘supported’ by the DPRK was involved in the incident,” said Kim Yo Jong said. “But the US assertion is an absurd rhetoric and nothing new.”

South Korea’s government has backed postponing tax on cryptocurrency gains by another two years to 2029. Judging on the troubled history of the tax, it would not be surprising if it is delayed again at that point.

Crypto.com will discontinue its consumer app services in Hong Kong on Feb. 1, 2027, while continuing to pursue regulatory approval for its institutional exchange.

A Crypto.com spokesperson told Cointelegraph that the company would discontinue its consumer app services in Hong Kong. The spokesperson said all affected customers had received advance notice and would receive instructions on closing their accounts and support to withdraw their funds.

Nearly three years after the local entity submitted an application for a license and the status remains “deemed-to-be-licensed” — which a transitional status rather than full regulatory approval.

The Hong Kong headquartered Animoca Brands has secured approval to manage investment funds for non-retail clients in Abu Dhabi.

The authorization, granted to subsidiary Animoca Asset Management (AD) Limited by Abu Dhabi Global Market’s Financial Services Regulatory Authority (FSRA), allows it to oversee funds that pool money from professional investors rather than the general public.

It expanding the asset management business of a company that began in mobile games before moving into blockchain gaming, rode the Metaverse wave and was a big player in venture investing.

Hong Kong has expanded its Project Ensemble pilot to allow round-the-clock settlement of tokenized transactions, as the city’s monetary regulator prepares to bring regulated stablecoins into trials involving tokenized government debt.

Hong Kong has recently officially introduced secondary market trading for SFC-authorised Tokenized Funds through licensed exchanges. Two China Asset Management money-market funds can now trade continuously via PantherTrade and the Futubull app.

Thailand’s securities regulator has filed a criminal complaint against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, for allegedly operating without a license. Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission will block access to the website and app from December 5.

According to the SEC, Bitget offered crypto trading services between 2022 and 2025, charged trading fees and promoted its services in Thai through its websites and social media channels. The regulator said these activities violated Thailand’s digital asset business licensing requirements.

The regulator advised users to take action regarding assets held on the platform before the deadline.

Australia’s central bank raised its interest rate by 25 basis points to 4.85% on Tuesday, warning that inflation would stay elevated through much of 2027 as energy costs and AI-related investment added to price pressures.

The Reserve Bank of Australia’s monetary policy board voted 6–3 for the increase, with three members preferring to hold rates at 4.60%.

The bank pushed back its forecast for inflation to return to the top of its 2% to 3% target range until late 2027, around three quarters later than previously expected.

The Australian government’s decision to scrap the 50% capital gains discount for all investments in the May budget came as a big shock for crypto owners. The new scheme effectively doubles the taxable component of a gain, minus a small discount for inflation.

Some Aussie crypto holders are taking drastic measures — like Finder founder and crypto king Fred Schebesta who is relocating to Singapore which offers 0% CGT, stating the tax changes were a “killer blow.”But there are less dramatic methods to reduce CGT bills including setting up a self managed super fund (SMSF), a company to trade crypto at the company tax rate, or to hope the opposition will reverse the changes after the next election.

Japanese yen stablecoin JPYC marked its first anniversary with a circulating supply around 3 billion yen—one quarter of its internal target.

BlackRock, BlackRock Japan, SBI Holdings and Startale have signed an MOU to explore connecting tokenized money-market funds with SBI’s JPYSC yen stablecoin.

Ministry of Finance rules require cryptocurrency exchanges licensed in Vietnam to report security incidents and remediation plan within 24 hours

Five major banks—including HSBC, JPMorgan’s Kinexys, MUFG, Société Générale-Forge under BNP Paribas, and Standard Chartered—have launched the Global Layer One Organization (GL1) in Singapore. The non-profit aims to promote standards for tokenized financial markets.

Cointelegraph is committed to providing independent, high-quality journalism across the crypto, blockchain, AI, and fintech industries.

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

Christopher Nolan's The Odyssey is returning to IMAX 70MM screens... again

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Christopher Nolan's The Odyssey is returning to the big screen... again. It's a re-return, if you will.

Per Deadline, The Odyssey will play on 1,000 screens, including IMAX and IMAX 70mm, in North America starting on December 11. This is three weeks after the nearly three-hour-long movie is set for a home release on premium VOD, 4K and DVD on November 17.

The film, which is no doubt going to sweep the Oscars, hit theaters on July 17, and stayed on premium large format screens until September 30 (including 4DX, because who doesn't want to be hit by waves for at least 60 minutes?). It was given a proper theatrical re-release that began on October 9 and lasted until the 31st. We don't know how long the December run will last.

Based on Homer's epic poem, The Odyssey follows Matt Damon's warrior Odysseus as he embarks on a decade-long journey home after the Trojan War, meeting up with some gods, goddesses, cyclops, and sirens along the way. The star-studded cast also includes Anne Hathaway as Odysseus's wife Penelope, Tom Holland, Robert Pattinson, Himesh Patel, Charlize Theron, Zendaya, Jon Bernthal, John Leguizamo, and Samantha Morton (yes, the very same from Mike Flanagan's Carrie).

The movie grossed over $1.8 billion at the global box office, making it the highest-grossing R-rated movie of all time and Universal's highest-grossing movie to date. It's what Homer would've wanted, honestly.

For more, check out our The Odyssey review, and our deep dives into The Odyssey monsters, The Odyssey poem, and The Odyssey ending explained.

Which WoW Forever ruleset to pick (and can you change later?)

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Instead of realms, you just choose a ruleset to play. It's a pretty way of saying "playstyle" - and World of Warcraft: Forever likes things pretty.

World of Warcraft: Forever opens your latest journey to Azeroth with a big first step, as logging in for the first time gives you a choice of rulesets. No sign of the ages-old realmlist, just one choice of four (soon five). In theory, this choice should be simple – but somehow it’s still a headscratcher. It’s like the stakes got bigger, somehow.

So there’s Normal, PvP, Roleplay, and Roleplay-PvP. The first two you’re already familiar with. And if you’re not used to seeing roleplay servers, this can be quite new (but it’s what the “RP” in MMORPG stands for). Is there a difference in the choices, though?

As it turns out, not a lot. But they’re significant.

As the name implies, the Normal ruleset is for anyone who wants to log into WoW: Forever after a busy workday and do whatever. Maybe you want to grind to a certain level, do a couple of raids, or just walk around Dalamar (the lighting is amazing now, thanks to visual upgrades).

If you’re playing WoW for the first time, or if you’re not really into PvP, you can choose Normal. For MMO vets, you’d recognize Normal as the standard-fare “PvE” default mode. This is where we think a huge portion of players will be during launch, and maybe even afterward.

PvP is available but at your discretion. It’s a separate setting you have to enable, and it only applies in Contested or Enemy Zones.

Adventuring around Azeroth is fun, but what if you want to test your build against other players? What if you’re a Horde player and you meet some punk from Stormwind? There’s no need to resist going berserk in PvP, because duels are actively encouraged here.

PvP is automatically enabled here, and there’s no going back. You’re always open to attack in Contested and Enemy Zones. There are also some new rules:

The main ethos behind the transition from Warcraft 3 into World of Warcraft was always living inside Azeroth. What if you lived as a character who just started a life of adventure after the Third War?

With the Roleplay ruleset, you do exactly that. If you’ve always imagined acting out your character and immersing yourself in Azeroth, this is the ruleset for you.

Roleplay gives a massive “space” for players who love the idea of playing their own characters (r/WoWRolePlay has been up since 2012, for instance). Its naming conventions are stricter (no puns, please) and players are expected to behave a certain way (mostly roleplay).

If you want to kick things up a notch, there’s Roleplay PvP. It’s similar to Roleplay, except PvP is actively encouraged. Expect a lot of drama and grief (both the emotion and being maliciously killed in the game) from the other faction.

Read what’s behind the latest news in gaming (with a bit of snark) by subscribing to the Destructoid Checkpoint newsletter!

There’s also a fifth “wildcard” ruleset that has yet to arrive in WoW: Forever, and that’s Hardcore.

As the name implies, Hardcore is for players who want a no-handholding experience. Naturally, Blizzard ups the ante for this challenge mode. It’s one strike, and you’re out.

But here’s what the difficulty mode means for the character specifically:

Unlike realms where each choice reveals a population density that preferred a “general type” of gameplay, rulesets in WoW: Forever have put a big emphasis on playstyle.

Remember, players in one ruleset likely enjoy and want to facilitate a specific playstyle. If you’ve gotten used to other players being in character in Roleplay, then Normal or PvP will be a jarring transition.

To avoid any conflict there, Blizzard put its foot down: no transfers between rulesets. If you want to play in another ruleset, you make a totally new character.

Likewise, you can’t play across rulesets. You’ll have to hop onto an alt in a different ruleset, say PvP, to play with your other friends in PvP. Same goes for the others.

Blizzard named one exception, and that’s Battlegrounds, which might include characters from different rulesets. No Hardcore, though.

There’s another catch, if “catch” was spelled “your name.”

When Blizzard introduced WoW: Forever, it also came with a big proposal: forging your legacy. Characters now come with first and second names, and no one else can have the exact two-name combination you picked for your character.

The idea is that, if you do something historic, everyone will know for sure that’s your character. Lore-wise, it also establishes a family name your future characters could use.

Naturally, this “exclusivity” component came with an early name reservation event between October 27 and November 3, 2026.

The big deal is that your choice of name is hard-set across all rulesets. That one name you picked in Normal can’t be used in PvP, Roleplay, and Roleplay-PvP.

If you’re not into roleplay, you’re good. But if you want a casual WoW experience and really sink your teeth into being an adventurer in Azeroth, well, that’s a tough choice.

Rulesets finally offer cleaner, much simpler choices for how to go about the World of Warcraft experience. At the end of the day, realms were only really important for two things:

If you don’t like fighting other players, stay away from PVP. Then high-population realms mean more chances of securing raid members all the time.

Now Forever makes this more intentional. Your playstyle takes precedence, and Blizzard handles everything else. So pick that ruleset and start grinding. Azeroth awaits.

Blizzard released World of Warcraft: Forever on November 4, 2026, for PC and macOS.

Ford’s electric pickup is suddenly selling like hotcakes at 50% off used

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Once America’s most popular electric pickup, the Ford F-150 Lightning is finding a second life on the used market.

The F-150 Lightning is hard to pass up at 50% to 60% off sticker price, even used. With new models no longer available, Ford’s electric pickup is suddenly selling like hotcakes on the used market.

After the last model rolled off the assembly line last December, CEO Jim Farley said the decision to end production came as the “very high-end EVs, the $50K, 70K, $80,000 vehicles, they just weren’t selling.”

At 50% to 60% off, it now looks like a bargain in the used market. “When Ford was selling it brand new, the math was complicated,” Jimmy Douglas, CEO of Plug, a wholesale EV dealer, recently told Bloomberg.

On the used market, however, “the Lightning is 50% to 60% of the sticker price and that is really a sweet spot,” he added.

Used Ford F-150 Lightnings are selling in about 20 days, less than half the 44-day average for used vehicles in the US. According to Edmunds.com, prices are now up 4% to about $50,000 since the start of the year.

Douglass said that close to one-third of used models are sold by rival dealerships that would normally flip them back to Ford. However, many are opting to sell them directly because of demand.

Chase Norris from Colorado called the electric pickup a bargain. He purchased a used Lightning for around $40,000 and considers it a major upgrade from his previous gas F-150.

EV Group CEO and cofounder Alex Lawrence, which sells pre-owned electric vehicles, said he can’t get his hands on any reasonably priced used F-150 Lightnings currently.

He said many Lightning buyers use it as a second truck to save money during their weekly commute.

It looks essentially the same as the gas-powered F-150, offers instant torque, and produces zero tailpipe emissions, but the electric pickup failed to meet Ford’s expectations because of its higher price.

The base F-150 Lightning Pro debuted in 2021 with a starting MSRP of $39,974, but that was quickly raised to around $60,000 a year or so later.

According to Edmunds.com, the average transaction price for the F-150 Lightning in its first year of sales was $79,000 because Ford focused on higher-priced trims. The gas version’s ATP was about $19,000 less.

Even with the higher sticker price, Ford was losing a considerable amount on each Lightning it produced. In Q1 2024, Ford lost more than $100,000 on every EV it built.

Next year, Ford will take another crack at the electric pickup market with the Fathom, the first vehicle based on its “groundbreaking” new Universal Electric Vehicle (UEV) platform.

Ford said the Fathom will start at $28,350 for the standard-range version. Even with the $1,595 delivery fee included, the electric pickup will start at under $30,000.

As the first vehicle built on its UEV platform, Ford promises the Fathom will be a step-change from its first-generation EVs.

According to Ford, the electric pickup will offer more interior space than a Toyota RAV4 and will be the first vehicle with Apple Maps directly integrated into the navigation system.

We also know the electric pickup will have a NACS port after a few prototypes were spotted charging at a Tesla Supercharger this summer.

Ford reportedly aims to sell around 100,000 Fathoms in its first year. CEO Jim Farley said on the company’s third-quarter earnings call last week that the launch remains on track for Q4 2027.

Farley called the Fathom a “breakthrough product” and said that the MSRP under $30,000 is perfect for today’s market.

At least some F-150 owners are already trading in for lower-priced options. A member of the Ford F-150 Lightning Owners Group, JF, said that although it was a great ride, “Carvana gave us just a little less than we paid new,” adding, “R2, here we come!”

When Torque News reached out, JF said he “really liked the Lightning,” but wanted something smaller. “With the current increase in resale value, the time to trade seemed right,” JF added.

The Rivian R2 (see our review) Performance with the Launch Package is now available to order starting at $57,990 with an EPA-estimated range of up to 330 miles and 656 hp. The R2 Premium will go on sale later this year, starting at $53,990

In Spring 2027, Rivian will launch the R2 Standard, starting at $44,990 with an estimated range of up to 275 miles and 350 hp.

So were they losing $100k / truck, or the average loss when all development was included ended up being $100k? Big difference. I don't believe the cost to build each one was $170k+. That makes no sense. They just priced them too high to amortize the development costs over enough vehicles to make them profitable. Every vehicle has to sell a minimum # before they start to make a profit.

Surging F-150 Lightning sales on the used market show buyers want electric pickups. But at the right price.

At $60,000 to $70,000, the Lightning wasn’t so attractive, but at $40,000 used, it suddenly is. Perhaps Ford is on to something with its next-gen EV pickup, the Fathom, set to go on sale starting at under $30,000.

Will it deliver on its promise? Ford has yet to reveal battery specs, towing capacity, range, or any other specs. We should learn more soon.

Jessica McClain's Approach to Running Breaks a Lot of the Usual Rules. It's Working.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Brooks athlete spoke to Women’s Health fresh off the NYC Marathon.

After a major marathon, you hear about two different podiums. There’s the actual podium, made up of the top three finishers, and then another, unofficial one of the fastest Americans. That’s because, though there’s some incredible talent in the U.S., oftentimes, it’s simply not enough to beat out international runners, including on our own courses. In the New York City Marathon women’s race, for example, an American hasn’t been in the top three for eight years. (In the men’s race, it’s been since 2009.)

That streak ended this Sunday when Brooks athlete Jessica McClain crossed the finish line in 2:24:42, coming in at second place. Even more exciting: She was poised to get third but overtook last year’s winner Hellen Obiri in the final meters. She did it looking effortless, with a pair of Goodr sunglasses in her hand and a huge smile on her face.

Even before the race cannon went off that morning, her momentum had been building. In the spring, McClain placed fifth in the Boston Marathon at 2:20:49 and set an American course record. Going into New York, she initially wanted to be in the top five again. “But then I was like, ‘Wait, I want to do better than I did in Boston, and that's fourth, and I do not want fourth place,’” she tells Women’s Health. So, it was settled: She was shooting for third or higher.

After a performance like she had in New York, you can expect that’s always going to be the goal now for the 34-year-old. “I think ‘top American’ just isn't going to cut it anymore for where I want to be on the international stage,” she says. “I want to compete and I want to practice competing, especially because we're coming up on the Olympic trials, and the top three go to the Olympics. I want to practice competing for those top three spots no matter the race.”

Heading into NYC, McClain was feeling relaxed. The race started out hard, she remembers, so she just focused on getting her fuel in at each aid station and taking the run mile by mile. “I was just checking boxes and staying out of trouble as much as I could the first 13 to 16 miles,” she says. Then, once she got to Manhattan, the pack started to run faster, her gels started to kick in, and she started to feel better. Still, it wasn’t until the last 10K when the running started to feel really good.

McClain was strategic until the end—running the tangents and taking the shortest turns possible to close the gap between Obiri and first-place finisher Sheila Chepkirui, who she noticed were making their turns a little wider and slower. Even down to the last stretch turning into Central Park, second wasn’t exactly on McClain’s mind. She was just thrilled to take third. She took off her sunglasses to take it all in, which is why they were in her hand as she crossed the finish line.

“Then I saw Hellen looking around and was like, ‘Oh my God, wait, I think I can get second,’” she says. So, she dug deep, pumped her arms, and fed off the crowd’s energy. “I just felt really, really good,” she says. “I've run a lot of reps at the end of a lot of hard workouts that were fast and I was like, ‘I can do this.’” The result was one of the most exciting second-place finishes to a marathon in a long time.

Despite her headline-grabbing performances in 2026, it was just a few years ago that McClain stepped away from professional running after hitting what she describes as “rock bottom.” She was chronically injured, having dealt with her share of tight tendons, stress fractures, plantar issues, and strained muscles, and unable to race at her full potential. So, she moved home to Arizona, got a full-time job in the nonprofit sector, and reevaluated her relationship with running.

“It gave me a lot of perspective,” McClain says. “For a long time I was very outcome oriented, not process driven. It was a lot about ‘We need to hit these times and get this place,’ and I wasn't enjoying the ride to get there, and it trickled into a lot of other things in life.”

From 2020 to 2022, McClain just ran for herself. She didn’t have a super formal plan or even a coach. She worked full time, met the man who is now her husband, reconnected with old friends, and had fun outside of her sport. “I needed that, but naturally found my way back [to running],” she says. She signed up for the Mesa Marathon in February 2022 to celebrate her 30th birthday—and won it.

Slowly, she started racing again, even self-coaching her way to a fourth-place performance in the 2024 Olympic marathon trials. Now, she’s resigned with Brooks and back to racing as a professional, but she still maintains all those other parts of herself she gained in her time off. That period away from professional running didn’t sabotage her progress. Instead, it set her up for this latest era, one where she’s happier—and more successful—than ever.

Stepping away from professional running led to a mindset shift for McClain. First, she prioritizes fun—and you can tell. Obviously, coming off of an incredible finish at one of the world’s biggest marathons will put you in a good mood, but it’s clear that McClain is enthusiastic about running. She leads with such a pure love for the sport that pros (and amateurs) can lose sight of, that she had once lost sight of until her time away.

“This is supposed to be fun,” McClain says. “Running is amazing, but at the end of the day, it's just running and there's so much else in life. I just want to enjoy it and to get the best out of myself and make the sport a little bit better, whatever that means.”

That philosophy carries over into her day-to-day training, where she prioritizes workouts she actually enjoys. “Eighty-five percent of the work I do gives me confidence, and then there's 15 percent that's really hard that I don't love, but that's good for me,” McClain says. She’s also not a cross-trainer. “It does not bring me joy,” McClain says. “I hate swimming. I hate biking.” Yes, even if she gets injured. When it comes to strength training, she keeps that minimal too.

Similarly, you’ll rarely catch McClain wrapping up a session doubled over and exhausted, she says. “I’m never hitting a home run in practice,” she says. “That’s for racing.”

Due to her full-time job as the executive director at the Johnjay & Rich #LoveUp Foundation, her weekly mileage also leans a little shorter than some of her competitors. (During this training block, McClain ran about 72 to 84 miles every week, with one hard session and one long run per week in addition to her easy miles.) Her coach, David Roche, also doesn’t assign runs based on paces. Instead, they focus on effort.

Beyond keeping training from getting too draining, McClain has seen another incredible benefit from this approach: She’s been injury-free for nearly four years. “That's something that just gives me a little bit of an edge,” she says. “I've had a lot of healthy blocks strung together.”

When it comes to nutrition, McClain takes the same refreshingly uncomplicated approach, and it’s paying off. “I just feel like I’m the strongest athlete I’ve ever been,” she says. It’s all very intuitive when it comes to deciding what to eat. You won’t catch her planning out meals or counting macros. There’s nothing super strict or specific to her diet. She eats what she wants and relies on snacks to keep her well-fed. “I’m never hungry,” she says.

Plus, her fueling plan doesn’t come in between her and living life. “In the off season and when my season isn't super crazy, I go out with friends, I have fun, I go to happy hour. I don't try to live in restriction or super high monitoring ever,” McClain says. When she gets closer to race day, she dials in a bit more, but since she’s been so relaxed otherwise, it just makes it fun to lock in for a month.

There is one food that’s become a mainstay in her diet: Burgers—two to three nights a week. “It's super easy,” McClain says. “Throw it on the grill, slap it between two buns and put whatever you want on it. It's great and I get a ton of protein.” Her sweet treat of choice is Dairy Queen, a spot she frequents.

Next, McClain has her sights set on the Olympics. She placed fourth at the last trials, making her an alternate for Paris but just missing the chance to race for the country.

In the meantime, as she waits for 2028, she’s going to keep crushing the marathon and competing for every podium. “It's taken a long time to get to the point where I look at these women—and they're women I've been watching and idolizing in the marathon for quite some time—and I'm like, ‘Oh no, I can race with them too,’” she says. But now, there’s no doubting that she can.

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.

Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday after the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank left roughly three million customers unable to access their accounts, turning a banking failure into a political emergency.

The announcement did not end the confrontation. Paraguay’s lower house voted 78-0 to impeach Peña minutes later, sending the charges to the Senate as Vice President Pedro Alliana prepared to take over, per AP.

The bank’s shutdown has also revived an argument familiar to Bitcoin holders: Money held by a financial intermediary is only as accessible as that institution allows. Self-custodied Bitcoin works differently, although it comes with risks of its own.

As the Bitcoin OGs would say, “Bitcoin fixes this.”

Paraguay’s central bank placed Ueno into resolution after regulators found its solvency ratio had fallen below half the legal minimum. The process, announced on November 3, followed accounting adjustments to the bank’s financial statements and left its app, cards, and transfers out of service.

Ueno claimed around 2.9 million customers, more than 60% of Paraguay’s adults. It also held roughly $800 million in public-sector deposits, including pension and health funds, according to AP, complicating an already painful freeze on ordinary savings and wages.

Peña previously held a stake in a company linked to Ueno’s owners, a relationship that fueled allegations of conflicts of interest. He has denied wrongdoing and said he sold his interest in March 2025, according to Reuters.

“My greatest wish is that the return of those funds be guaranteed, that the Deposit Guarantee Fund fulfill its purpose,” Peña said in his resignation address. The Deposit Guarantee Fund would be the equivalent of the FDIC in the United States.

Mensaje del Presidente de la República, Santiago Peña, al pueblo paraguayo. pic.twitter.com/RBz6IfFV9L

— Santiago Peña (@SantiPenap) November 5, 2026

Outside Congress, the anger was less diplomatic. “Santiago Peña and the Colorado Party have stolen all our savings,” protester Luis Pineda told AP. “We are fed up with 70 years of plunder.”

The central bank says Ueno’s failure is an isolated case, not evidence of a broader banking collapse. It has announced a deposit guarantee of up to 228.3 million guaraníes per depositor, roughly $36,000, with initial payments targeting eligible accounts holding no more than 10 million guaraníes.

Bitcoin was born during another banking crisis. Its first block, mined in January 2009, included a newspaper headline about a second bailout for British banks, a historical detail long cited by supporters who distrust reliance on financial intermediaries.

A Bitcoin holder who controls a wallet’s private keys can authorize transfers from the wallet without asking a third party, like a bank, to unlock an account. That is the principle behind the crypto saying “not your keys, not your coins,” and the distinction from leaving Bitcoin on an exchange that can itself freeze withdrawals.

But Bitcoin is not perfect. Self-custody has its own risks: Forget your keys or lose them and there is no way you can recover your funds, there is nobody who can assist you, and no law enforcement agency who can do anything for you.

Paraguay’s central bank said Thursday its first guarantee payments would go to depositors with up to 10 million guaraníes, no Ueno debts and an account at another financial institution.

Rings Of Power’s Season 3 Release Schedule Confused Me, But The Reason For It Makes Sense

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

New And Recent Movies Streaming: Big Films We Can Finally Watch At Home - Updated November 2026

I Can't Decide If I Love Or Hate Matt Dinniman's Dungeon Crawler Carl TV Show Update

I'm So Bummed About Daredevil: Born Again's Cancellation But Charlie Cox's POV Makes Me Feel Better

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power is gearing up to make its return on the 2026 TV schedule, and fans should mark their calendars because the release schedule for Season 3 is a bit confusing. The fantasy show isn't doing traditional weekly releases, and it's not going full-binge either; instead, it will be taking a page out of Netflix’s Stranger Things handbook and releasing episodes in blocks. While this does give me a headache, the showrunner's reasoning for it makes perfect sense.

Those with an Amazon Prime subscription will be treated to the first four episodes of Rings of Power Season 3 on November 11. A week later, on November 18, two more episodes will be released. Then, the final two episodes will be released on November 25. In the past, this show has dropped a chunk of episodes at the start and then released one at a time on a weekly basis. However, this model they're using for Season 3 is quite different, and it will give fans the show very fast. It sounds like getting the streaming service to agree to the schedule was a bit of a challenge, as co-showrunner Patrick McKay told io9:

We’re always in conversation with Amazon, and we’re advocating for what we think is best for the show.

It’s not the first time showrunners have had to advocate for a different release strategy with their streaming services. Just last year, the Duffer Brothers sat down with Netflix to persuade them into releasing the final episode of Stranger Things in theaters alongside the streaming service drop. Plus, that final season was released in three chunks between Thanksgiving and the new year. McKay and his co-showrunner J.D. Payne weren’t gunning for a theatrical release, but they did feel strongly about being able to release the show in blocks instead of rolling out episodes weekly, as McKay continued:

We generally, as the creators, feel that the show plays better on a binge because it really is so highly serialized and there are so many storylines. It’s the kind of show, just as viewers, that we find that you really have to pay attention to and you want to get that feeling you get when you’re hooked on a show where it sweeps you away.

As much as I think the binge-watching model is ruining television, I can see why a block release makes sense for Rings of Power. The show is packed with storylines, and they can't all be addressed in every episode, meaning fans would sometimes have to wait weeks before they saw their favorite characters back on screen. That won’t be the case this year, which will be a welcome change, especially after the revelation about what The Stranger’s actual name is.

Season 3 will also feature a time jump, which means there’s going to be even more for fans to catch up on. In an age when everyone’s attention spans are so short, it makes sense that a block schedule would be used so fans don’t forget minor details that might be important in subsequent episodes, which is exactly the point McKay was making, saying:

And we find when you watch it, boom, boom, boom, boom, it sort of works better. So we’re always advocating for more episodes sooner, and then this particular season, we really wanted it to be out by the holidays. We wanted people to be able to binge it over the holidays. So it’ll be a quick release, but then you can watch it at your leisure.

In a way, The Rings of Power release schedule has something for all kinds of television watchers. Fans who want to watch things immediately or prefer a weekly release can engage in mini-binge sessions every seven days. Meanwhile, fans who prefer an hours-long traditional binge session only have to wait three weeks for the entire show to be released instead of six to eight weeks.

It will be interesting to see how the new release schedule affects its viewership, but The Lord of the Rings fandom is huge, and Season 4 is already in development, so I’m sure it will be fine.

If you haven't been watching the show, or need to brush up before Season 3 begins, now is the time to do it!

Lionsgate Quarterly Revenue Pops 41% on Strength of ‘Michael,’ TV Series Sales as Studio Narrows Net Loss

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Lionsgate turned in another strong quarter — helped again by the studio’s blockbuster biopic on the King of Pop.

For the three months ended Sept. 30, Lionsgate’s Q2 of fiscal 2026, the company reported revenue of $668.8 million, up 41% year over year. Operating income came in at $29.1 million, versus an operating loss of $46.0 million a year ago.

Net loss from continuing operations attributable to shareholders of $15.5 million, or a diluted net loss per share of 5 cents. Related Stories Guillermo del Toro Tears Up as He Says ‘We’re Handing Down a World in Ruins’: ‘It Pains Me Deeply to See Three Generations Fail the Next’ Japan IP Market to Return to Cannes in 2027 as Tokyo Partnership Continues

“Our strong performance in the quarter reflects all of the pieces of our fiscal 2027 plan coming together, reaffirming our confidence in this year’s forecast of significant adjusted OIBDA [Operating income before depreciation and amortization] growth,” Lionsgate CEO Jon Feltheimer said in prepared remarks. “We had very positive momentum in the quarter as we continued to set in place our film tentpoles for the next three years. Combined with another great library performance and continued encouraging signs in our broader operating environment, we have growing visibility into our continued growth trajectory in fiscals 2028 and 2029.”

Lionsgate’s trailing 12-month library revenue was $1.04 billion — the studio’s second highest ever. Motion picture segment revenue increased 30% year-over-year to $359.3 million, and segment profit increased significantly year-over-year to $88.7 million. The results were driven by the ongoing contribution of “Michael,” which grossed over $1 billion at the box office, and continued library strength.

Television Production segment revenue increased 56% year-over-year to $309.5 million, and segment profit increased 73% versus the prior year to $21.6 million. The results were driven by what Lionsgate said was a significant increase in scripted episodic deliveries, including “Power: Origins” and the second seasons of “The Studio” (for Apple TV) and “The Hunting Wives” (for Netflix).

The company said it anticipates doubling scripted deliveries in fiscal 2027 relative to fiscal 2026.

Ten of Lionsgate’s next 15 wide theatrical releases “are repeatable branded properties,” Feltheimer said on the company’s earnings call. “With the new ‘Hunger Games,’ ‘Resurrection of the Christ’ Parts 1 & 2, ‘The Housemaid’s Secret,’ ‘Michael 2’ and ‘Naruto’ leading the way, complemented by other marquee properties in proved areas of strength, we have confidence in the earnings power of our film slate over the next three years.”

“The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” opens in two weeks to kick off the Thanksgiving box office, and, Feltheimer touted that, “We believe that this will be one of our biggest and best Hunger Games yet.”

On the TV business, Feltheimer cited “The Rookie,” which “is growing in popularity as it heads into its ninth season.” “The Rookie: North” spinoff will arrive in January on ABC and will stream on Hulu in the U.S. and Disney+ in most international territories. In addition, “Ghosts” is exploring new spinoff opportunities as it enters its sixth season on CBS. And Lionsgate’s recent licensing deal with Netflix “is indicative of the kind of value we’re creating for the Power franchise 10 years after the original Power series launched.”

Netflix announced the third season renewal of “The Hunting Wives” before its second season launched. Season 2 of the show debut on Thanksgiving Day, and Lionsgate will begin shooting the third season at the Lionsgate Studios sound stages in Atlanta next month.

All Great Fairy's Fountain locations in Ocarina of Time

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Great Fairy's Fountains are home to, well, Great Fairies in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake. The Great Fairies are your gateway to magic and abilities. They'll give you spells that let you create fire, teleport in and out of dungeons, and grant yourself temporary invulnerability.

However, the Great Fairies are hidden away across Hyrule, and they're pretty hard to find. That's not all though, as in the remake, you have to offer the fairies rupees before they'll provide you with any gifts.

Here's where to find every Great Fairy Fountain in the Ocarina of Time remake, as well as what abilities you'll receive and how many rupees you have to offer to each Great Fairy.

There are six Great Fairy's Fountains in Ocarina of Time, and each Great Fairy will provide you with a unique reward if you can provide them with enough rupees.

The Great Fairies can be found in the following locations:

For their exact locations, check out the sections below.

The first Great Fairy's Fountain can be found at the top of Death Mountain Trail, but you'll first need to unlock the Bomb Bag from Dodongo Cavern.

After you have the Bomb Bag, take the path that takes you toward Death Mountain Crater, which is the easternmost path on Death Mountain Trail. Blow up the rocks blocking your way up, and make your way to the stone arch at the top. Go to the end of the tunnel, and the volcano will erupt, launching molten rocks onto the path in front of you. Avoid the rocks and run up the hill as fast as you can. Take out the three Skullwalltulas on the wall, and climb to the top.

On your left, you'll find Kaepora Gaebora and a breakable rock wall behind him. Blow up the wall and play Zelda's Lullaby on the Triforce inside. The Great Fairy will speak to you, and tell you that she'll grant you a new power if you put 25 rupees into the fountain.

Pay her 25 rupees, and you'll unlock magic power and Din's Fire. Magic power is a resource used to cast spells, like Din's Fire. Din's Fire costs two magic power, and when you cast it, fire will spread outwards, dealing fire damage to anything nearby.

The second Great Fairy's Fountain is located on the way to Hyrule Castle, but it's only reachable by young Link. Additionally, like the previous Great Fairy's Fountain, you need the Bomb Bag from Dodongo Cavern.

After you climb up the vines and go past the first gate, you'll see a rock wall on the right side of the path. Break it with a bomb and you'll find a tunnel that leads to the Great Fairy.

This time, you need to offer her 50 rupees, but she'll give you Gift of Improved Spin. Now, you can hold down the attack button to charge up a spin attack with magic power. The attack itself won't cost any magic power, but it'll deal additional damage.

The third Great Fairy's Fountain is located at the Zora Fountain. To enter, you'll need the Bomb Bag from Dodongo Cavern.

After you get King Zora to move, head behind him and you'll enter Zora Fountain. Go to the southeast corner of the map, and blow up the rock wall in the corner. Enter the cave, and you'll find the next Great Fairy.

Offer the Great Fairy 100 rupees, and you'll learn Farore's Wind, a spell that allows you to create a warp point in a dungeon for two magic power.

The fourth Great Fairy's Fountain is found in Death Mountain Crater, but first, you need to complete the Fire Temple and unlock the Megaton Hammer. Lastly, make sure you're in the future, and you have the Goron Tunic equipped.

From the Fire Temple warp point, head across the bridge to the east and cross the gap. Follow the path to the south, and you'll see a black rock embedded in the wall. Break it with the Megaton Hammer, and you'll discover the entrance to the Great Fairy's Fountain.

Give her 200 rupees, and she'll give you the Gift of Doubled Magic, which will double your magic power bar.

The fifth Great Fairy's Fountain is located in Evil Desert Colossus, which is essentially the entrance to the Spirit Temple. You'll reach this area after traveling through the Mirage Desert.

Break the rock wall on the north side of the zone, and you'll find the entrance to the Great Fairy's Fountain.

Put 300 rupees into the fountain, and you'll receive Nayru's Love, which will make you temporarily invulnerable for four magic power.

The sixth and final Great Fairy's Fountain is just outside of Ganondorf's Castle, but first, you need the Golden Gauntlets from the Shadow room inside the castle.

Before you cross the bridge into Ganondorf's Castle, you'll find a huge black and gold rock to the east. Pick it up and throw it away to find the entrance to the Great Fairy's Fountain.

Offer the Great Fairy 500 rupees, and you'll receive the Gift of Halved Damage. This will literally halve the amount of damage you receive from any damage source.

How to find Richard the dog in Ocarina of Time

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

How to identify Richard and complete the quest

Finding Richard is a side quest in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake. You might have seen Richard, the adorable dog, during your first visit to Hyrule Castle Town: a white energetic dog that you couldn’t pet because its owner would accuse you of trying to steal her dog. If you visit the town again later, you will learn that Richard has disappeared.

Luckily, you can help the distressed owner to find her precious dog. Below, we explain how to find Richard in the Ocarina of Time remake.

To find Richard, you must visit Hyrule Castle Town during the night and identify it among the running dogs. To know which one is Ricard, you must look for a white dog that will twirl and emit a high-pitched bark when you interact with it. All the other dogs have a deeper bark and briefly stand on their hind legs instead of spinning like Richard does. Since you can only look for Richard at night, consider unlocking the Sun's Song to change the time.

While it is possible to find Richard through trial and error, you don't need to. First look for the white dogs, then interact with them to see how they react. Since there aren't many white dogs, checking each one does not take much time. When I found Richard, he was on the street on the left side of the map, but I can't guarantee that it would always be in the same place for every player.

Once you have interacted with and identified Richard, he will follow you. Head to the street that leads to the entrance of the city to find its owner, then talk to her. After a detailed analysis, she will confirm that that dog is, indeed, Richard, and she will reward you with a Piece of Heart.

If you want to start this quest but Richard hasn't disappeared yet, be sure to deliver Zelda's letter to the guard in Kakariko Village after completing the "Princess of Hyrule Castle" quest. Progressing through the campaign until this point will trigger a change in Hyrule Castle Town and unlock the Richard quest.

Blue Origin's New Glenn launch delay will continue into 2027

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Repairs have been slow after an explosion earlier this year.

Blue Origin made waves earlier this year after a New Glenn rocket exploded on its launchpad in May 2026. This issue during a hot-test caused notable damage to the company's infrastructure in Florida that has delayed it from further activity. Shortly after the incident, Blue Origin's CEO claimed that the repairs would be finished quickly and that the next launch would occur before the end of the year. According to an internal memo from CEO Dave Limp, however, that timeline has been pushed back. The next rocket launch is now not expected until January 2027. Financial Times first reported on this development.

The memo suggests that the launchpad's Vehicle Access Tower has been repaired and restacked, and the launch bridge has been restored. Limp credited weather issues for the fixes happening slower than hoped. "Your collective work on our return to flight has been nothing short of remarkable," he wrote. "In just six months, Team Blue has accomplished what many thought would take years."

The company, owned by Jeff Bezos, is on the verge of notably expanding its footprint in space once it is cleared for takeoff again. Blue Origin won a contract from NASA in September to set up a telecommunications network for Mars. Its New Glenn rockets are also slated to bring more gear into space to support Amazon's Leo satellite broadband service.

Inside the NYC Members' Club Where Republicans and Democrats Party Together

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A half-dozen of us are squeezed into a small service elevator, our various colognes clashing with the signature fish-and-rubber scent of Lower Manhattan. The elevator operator blares Chinese ballads, and the young man next to me bobs his head in appreciation. An older man shifts uncomfortably. “Could you actually make it louder?” he snarks. Over the course of one excruciatingly slow ride to the sixth floor, we’re trapped in our private discomfort or pleasure. But we’re all headed to the same place: a midterms watch party at The Bench, a members’ club for people who are into politics but feel like political outsiders. It’s a place, according to its website, where “restless organizers, creatives, misfits, sneakerheads, and policy nerds collide.”

Since its founding two years ago, The Bench has hosted insiders ranging from CBS News editor-in-chief Bari Weiss to New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani. Monthly dues, “just to keep the lights on,” run as little as $50. The space is regularly used for events unaffiliated with The Bench: readings by literary magazines, climate week pop-ups, splashy debates. Michka Bengio, the social club’s owner and co-founder, is an earnest believer in dialogue and, if his programming is any indication, a little bit of a chaos agent. He likes to produce scenarios that underscore political tensions, like when he had Weiss’s The Free Press, co-host a watch party for Mamdani’s 2025 election. According to those who attended, it was a somber evening for the pro-Cuomo crowd, though some of them nevertheless returned to The Bench for this week’s midterms viewing.

On the night of the midterm elections, partygoers had the option of viewing the election results in a spacious, orderly room displaying CNN’s broadcast or in a lounge-y, narrower space screening Fox News’s coverage. In a cozy hallway between the two screens, guests could buy cocktails and popcorn or engage with people whose political views differ from their own—arguably The Bench’s most exclusive offering.

“There’s just a whole splatter of different opinions that come in here,” says David Azoulay, who works with the content agency Fixer Media. “The Free Press, the Manhattan Institute, and then I also get to meet a lot of people who are working in the Democratic space, on the Biden or Kamala Harris campaigns.” Azoulay helped produce events for an “America 250” tour in collaboration with Young America’s Foundation, a conservative youth organization, and has worked with provocateur Ben Shapiro and former Prime Minister Boris Johnson. Though he used to be a registered Democrat, he “grew up” and now identifies as a proud Republican. While Azoulay engaged in a playful back-and-forth with a progressive 21-year-old over what he considers to be the fallacy of socialism, Democratic councilmember Christopher Marte milled about wearing an “I Voted” sticker.

Over the sound of people screaming about the James Talarico-Ken Paxton race, Marte told me he met Bengio 10 years ago through Arena, a New York-based political organization that took off in the wake of the first Trump presidency. “It was all these young people trying to figure out how to get involved in politics. A.O.C. was on the couch, Alessandra Biaggi, all these people who were just curious, post-Trump election,” he says. At the time, Bengio was working as a campaign manager for Michael Basch, a techno-optimistic Democratic NYC mayoral candidate, a gig he secured by asking the only person he knew in politics to introduce them. He’s also worked as a consultant for NBA Africa and managed downtown restaurants like Regina’s Grocery and Miss Lily’s. The Bench is one of four Bengio ventures; he also co-founded the supper club Shtick with his wife, Jacqueline Lobel, and partners with the Hancock Foundation and the Hancock Cafe. On any given event night, he can be found buzzing around the room, excitedly facilitating introductions for his guests.

The son of a Moroccan father and Algerian mother, Bengio grew up in Strasbourg, France. When I ask what motivated him to move to New York in 2001, he deadpans: “Ever heard of the American dream?” More specifically, though, he was jealous of a friend who moved to the States for six months and returned speaking fluent English. “He was the coolest kid,” Bengio says. “And I was like, ‘I want to go to America, too.’ I love basketball,” he says, reorganizing a pile of books so that the edges aligned just so.

Bengio, a first-time voter, is coy about his own politics. Instead, he’s driven by the promise of America “that you can coexist,” he says, and a desire to nurture civil, democratic debate. “I want to be part of the project.”

“We need to really recommit and reaffirm what we mean when we talk about free speech—expression that is unencumbered by political interference in any way, shape, or form,” says Josef Palermo, a former curator at the Kennedy Center who drove up from the nation’s capital to attend this party. Earlier this year, Palermo wrote about the politicization of the Kennedy Center by both the Biden and Trump administrations in an article for The Atlantic. “We need spaces like this in D.C. Everybody’s in their own camps and certain venues are definitely coded one way or the other. I think having something like this would be huge in our nation’s capital.”

Then again, it’s partly New York’s distance from the D.C. swamp that makes talking about politics at The Bench feel cool. The space is decorated with Bengio’s enviable collection of NBA and political memorabilia. (He jokes that he stays up late scrolling vintage sites and bidding on Americana rarities.) Alongside Michael Jordan Wheaties boxes and a slew of rare basketball sneakers, there’s a framed copy of Emile Zola’s 1897 letter to the French youth during the Dreyfus affair, a portable Florida voting booth from the Bush vs. Gore election, a framed paper shift dress from Richard Nixon’s 1968 presidential campaign, and nearly every issue of John F. Kennedy Jr’s George magazine. The one with a Donald Trump cover gets taken down from the wall often, Bengio says, but he always puts it back up.

On Tuesday night, there appeared to be other collectors of rare political merch. Vintage Obama-era crop tops caught my eye, and a speechwriter for a New York City council member waxed poetic about his favorite vinyl record: a collection of John F. Kennedy speeches. Two guys wore t-shirts they designed themselves that cheekily quoted Michigan turncoat Haley Stevens: “Israel cums to me in my dreams.” (One of them requested anonymity, fearful of being cancelled by the Canary Mission, an anonymous organization that tracks critics of Israel.)

A good number of attendees who were professionally invested in the election outcomes couldn’t stop checking their phones. Anthony DiMieri, the man behind Mamdani’s winning social media strategy, told me he’d just come from Brad Lander’s party. Max Altman, the brother of the OpenAI founder Sam, also stopped by. He’s excited about a recent move to New York from Austin, Texas, and if he hadn’t forgotten to request an absentee ballot, he says, he would’ve voted for James Talarico.

As the results turned progressively blue, the room began to clear out. Before leaving, I chatted with Azoulay’s “libertarian friend” Hunt Beaty. In the hallway between broadcasts, Beaty tells me he’s not terribly moved by the midterms. “Everything interesting that is happening in the world right now, to me, is happening outside of politics,” he says. He rejects the libertarian designation and lives by the credo “free minds and free markets,” citing Reason magazine. And yet, Beaty finds that The Bench still has something to offer him.

“It’s fun to be in a room filled with buzzy, interesting people who at least are able to try and talk about ideas, while also being like, ‘Maybe we’re doing coke in the bathroom, too.’ I want there to be a New York City where those things could coexist.”
